My dear Dr. Dennett:
3451
In a study that I am pursuing relative
to Magdalena Bay and the Lodge Resolution I find
814.
repeated references to a denial by both the Japanese
and the Mexican governments of any hidden designs.
The volumes of Foreign Relations for the years l9ll
and 1912 do not contain these statements, or, for
that matter, any reference to the entire subject.
It may be that these denials were oral, but if they
were not, I should greatly aporeciate securing copies
of them, or any other correspondence, not already
published, relative to the subject. I am of course
familiar with the material published in the Senate
Documents for that period.
I shall naturally be glad to re iburse you
for any expense incident to this work.
JAN 2 J 1931
Very sincerely yours,
FILED
Thomas Q. Bailey

sir:
With referenoe to your letter of January 5, 1931,
relating to the Magdalema Bay inoident in 1912, you
are informed that a searoh of this Department's files
fails to disclose any written communications on the
subject erchanged, at that time at least, between the
Governments conoerned.
From the records it appears that on or about April 5,
1912, official statements were given for publication to
the NEW YORK TIMES and the UNITED PRESS by the Japanese
Government and the Mexioan Government respectively; that
these statements were, on the part of Japan, orally con-
firmed both by the Japanese Embassy in Washington and by
the

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.92)
-2
the Fereign Office in Tokyo; and that subsequently the
Japanese Ambassador, with his Government's authorisatiom
and merely for the information of the Department of State,
orally made an unreserved and categorical denial of the
rumored purohase of land at Magdalena Bay by the Japanese
Government or by a Japanese oompany, oharacterising the
repart as entirely sensational and utterly without any
foundation whatever.
This letter is written for your information and it
1s requested that you do not quote or refer to its contents
as coming from the Department of State.
Very truly yours,

I am preparing a volume on Certain Yncidents in
the Pacific Coast diplomacy of the United States, among
which I am including the Magdalena Bay episode (i9l2) and
the resulting Lodge resolution.
Senator Lodge himself wrote some years later that
his resolution had subsequently been invoked by the Depart-
ment of State. The only incident of this nature that I
have been able to find thus far is referred to in the New
York Times for March 22 and 31, 1919. It 1s here stated
that the Department of State,two weeks berore,specifically
referred the California and Mexican Land Company of Los D
Angeles to the Lodge resolution and a message of President
143
FILED
Taft preceding it, the occasion being the rumor that the
company was about to dispose of its holdings in Lower
6
California to Japanese subjects.
9
3
894.20212
I expect to make of this item in preparing my
study, but I should feel a good deal more confident in
citing it if the Department of State were willing to assure
me that the facts here stated are substantially correct.
Naturally, if it is so desired, I would not mention con-
firmation by the Department.
If you know of any other instances when the Lodge
resolution was invoked, I should greatly apnreciate your
suggesting them to me.
Thanking you for past favors, I am,
Very sincerely yours,
Thowas @. Bailey

CONFIDENTIAL.
SUBJECT:
Japone Sampams Cruising Along West
1—1055
Coast of México.
ans 'd

FAN EASTN AFFAIRS

THE HONORABLE
894.20212/310

THE SECRETARY OFSEA
note
894.20212
WASHINGTON
Stator
SIR:
I have to inform the Department that four
Japanese sampams called at the port of Mazatlán
on March 29, 1931, enroute north into the Gulf
of California.
While these sampams were here the man in
E
charge, Mr. Naotaro Kobayashi, Comisionado del
PLTED
Ministerio Transoceánico del Imperio del Japón,
MAY5
Tampico 29, México, D. F., claimed they had a
concession for pearl fishing along the west coast
of Mexico, and that they were engaged in pearl
fishing, having begun at Acapulco and were working
their way north, it being necessary for them to
put into Mazatlán for provisions and other supplies.
Some of the employees of conmercial houses which
furnished provisions to these sampams went on board
to make delivery of purchases have stated that no
pearl fishing equipment was visible at the time of
their visits on board.
It has been unofficially reported from various
sources, in fact it is a common rumor here that these
boats
ME

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
boats were not interested in pearl fishing, but
were making a survey of the entire west coast,
charting and mapping all coves, inlets and harbors.
It is rumored that these sampams surveyed the coast
north as far as Guaymas, and intended to go farther
up the Gulf of Lower California, and to return down
the east coast of the peninsula of Lower California,
then go north up the west coast of the Peninsula
as far as Ensenada.
Usually when a pearl fishing fleet arrives at
Mazatlán an attempt is made to dispose of some of
their pearls, or at least some of the pearls are
exhibited, but so far as this office has been able
to ascertain no pearls belonging to this fleet were
exhibited; however, that may have been due to the
secretive character of the Japanese. It is possible
that Mr. Naotaro Kobayashi may have a concession
for pearl fishing along the west coast, and they may
have been doing some pearl fishing, but from circum-
stances surrounding their visit here, and to the fact
that they have been visiting every little cove and
inlet, it would appear that the main object of their
expedition was to make a minute and detailed survey
and chart of the entire west coast of México.
Respectfully yours,
E. W. Eaton,
American Vice Consul.

894.20212/312
My dear Mr. Secretary:
On November 11, 1931, Mr. T. B. Doolittle, Mounted Customs
Inspector, at Yuma, Arizona, advised an Agent of the Bureau of
Investigation of this Denartment that on or about October 8, 1931,
thirty Japanese engineers landed at Guaymas, Sonora, Mexico, and
had been manping roads over the West Coast of that state, making a
topographical survey of the country. They are equipped with sur-
veyor instruments and are enga ed in preparing maps. No information
is available at this time s to whether these Japanese are Govern-
ment or private engineers, or whether same involves a commercial
enterprise.
The  bove information as forwarded to the Secretary of
the Navy as of possible interest to that Depertment, and under cate
of December 9, 1931, a request vas received from that Deoartment
that further investiration be concucted in this matter. For your
informaticn, a copy of the letter received from the Honorable C. F.
Adams, Secretary of the Navy, is being attached hereto.
The Bureau of Inve: tigation is being authorized to concuct
FILFD
an investigation of this matter, again contacting Mr. Doolittle, the
informant. The investigation to be conducted will, of cource, be
limited to the confines of the United States. The above information
6
is being referred to you as a matter of possible interest to your
3
2
Department.
Very truly youre,
Attorney General.
Enclosure #734834.

9 December 1931
A8-5(311204)
My dear Mr. Attorney General:
Your letter No. R.ST.L. - 235025, of 4 December
has been received by this Department with considerable in-
terest. It would be much appreciated if your special agent
at Phoenix, Arizona, throug bis contacts could furnish
further information concerning the various activities of the
Japanese engineers in Sonora, Mexico. It is particularly
desirable to have information of the areas covered by the
topographical survey. So far the Department has been unable
to locate the town of Cabosca, Sonora. Any information tend-
ing to its definite location woula oe helpful.
Sincerely yours,
C. F. ADAMS (Sga.)
Honorable William D. Mitchell,
Attorney General,
Washington, D. C.

December 9, 1931
MEMORANDUM
RECF
From:
To :
Chief of Naval Operations.
JAN 23 1032
SUBJECT:
Japanese activities in Mexico andDatisvariou
Enclosure:
(a) Secret report from Military Intelligence
Division on Japanese activities inimical to
the United States.
1. The Attorney General has just forwarded the following
information from their special agent in Phoenix, Arizona:
That Louis Collins informant of Mr. Doolittle (Mounted
Customs Inspector, Yuma, Arizona), living at Cabosca. Sonora,
Mexico, stated that on or about October 8, 1931, 30 Japanese
-engineers - landed at Guaymas, Sonora, Mexico, and had
been mapping roads all over the West Coast of the State of
Sonora, Mexico, making a topographical survey of the country.
They are equipred with surveyor instruments preparing maps.
After their arrival at Guaymas, Sonora, they divided in
1
groups of two and three and went in different directions.
Two of these Japanese were stationed at Cabosca, Sonora on
Lower California Bay. Appear to be well educated men, and
talk good Spanish, English and German. Up to November 1,
1931 they were sti11 in Sonora.
2. In connection with this report, attention is invited to
Enclosure (a). A recapitulation of other reports concerning
Japanese activities is as follows:
The Editor of the Seattle Post-Intelligencer furnished
the following information to Arthur Brisbane on 29 August,
193l: The information in question was originally furnished
by Paul Buckley, one of the Directors of the Pacific Coast
Sulphur Comnany of Seattle, Washington. Mr. Buckley from
1914 until 1922 was a Deputy U. S. Marshal stationed at
Dutch Harbor, Unalaska. About 1915 or 1916 a ship wrecked
Japanese crew on Nunidak Island was picked up and brought
down to Dutch Harbor. At an investigation which was con-
ducted by the Captain of the U. S. Coast Guard Cutter the
Japanese disclaimed any knowledge of English. Later on it
was discovered that some of the crew spoke English readily.
amongst whose effects was found a much better chart or map
of Nunidak Island than was in the possession of the Coast
Guard.
In 1931 Mr. Buckley was in Dutch Harbor and encountered
there a Japanese boat which had brought over supplies and
gasoline

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
gasoline for distribution to points along the Aleutian
Islands for a proposed flight from Tokyo to Seattle.
Several of the Japanese connected with this flight were
on steamers with Mr. Buckley on his trips to various points
in Alaska. One claimed to be a Rear Admiral and both he
and the others were constantly studying maps, taking pictures
and making notes on charts both Japanese and U. S. charts.
The Commander Bering Sea Patrol Force reported to the
Treasury Department as of 1 October, 193l, the following
Japanese activities in connection with the proposed good-
wiil flight across the Pacific: A Japanese trawler during
the month of May visited Attu, Atka, and Unalaska. The party
on this trawler showed great familiarity with the water
around Attu, and came in the narrow entrance tc Attu Harbor,
which has a depth of only 14 feet, on a dark night at good
speed. A party was landed at Attu, one of whom was a camera-
man, who took pictures both still and movie until he ran out
of filns. Rock specimens were gathered, but no soundings
were taken although the previous year the Japanese had
sounded out Holtz Bay. During the winter 1929 and 1930 the
Japanese had evidently used the Island of West Semichi near
Attu as the natives found a target there with used cartridges
cases and a Japanese tea cup.
At Atka another party of three was landed consisting of
a correspondent, a Botanist, and a cameraman. The Botanist
was very busy collecting specimens while the cameraman took
pictures from all angles and from the mountains on either
side of the Bay.
At Unalaska a party of four was landed, one of whom was
a correspondent, another represented himself to be an aero-
nautical expert and a Captain in the Japanese Navy, another
a. scientist connected with the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries,
and another professional cameraman. The latter was well
supplied with films for both still and movie picture cameras,
and took pictures during his entire stay of all activities
including the new dock under construction and the arrival
and departure of every vessel, Coast Guard, Mavy or Merchant-
man, also panoramic views were taken from the high mountains
in the Unalaska locality. The Commanding officer of the
trawler had the rank of a Lieutenant-Commander in the Japan-
ese naval reserve, the Chief officer was on detached duty
from one of the large Japanese mercantile lines. It is re-
ported radiomen were also on board who stood continuous
watches and were of a hi er type than is ordinarily found
on a trawler.
The following is an extract from the letter dated 23
March, 1931 of Mr. Armour addressed to the Director of
Naval Intelligence. Four Sampans arrived at Manzanillo on
a

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
a Japanese steamer. These boats were small, too well
fitted out for fishing purposes and had no cold storage
apparatus for fish preservation, and apparently did not
have sufficient capacity for any great quantity of fish.
At the stern of each was a brass column used for housing
propellers. A partially covered theodolite was observed
on one of them. Each boat was manned by a crew of five
Japanese who appeared to be of a type superior to that of
fishermen. They claimed to have a fishing concession in
Mexico which later developed not to be a concession, but
merely a permit similar to that given to sportsmen. These
same boats were encountered later on at Mazatlan and Guaymas,
although they had left Manzanillo prior to Mr. Armour's
departure they did not arrive at Mazatlan until two days
after Mr. Armour's arrival although the yacht of the latter
has a very slow speed.
The Manager of the Royal Hawaiian Corporation, Hilo
Beacn in a letter of 31 December, 1930, to Governor Lawrence
M. Judd, of Hawaii stated as follows: I feel bound to in-
form you that the Government of Japan makes their naval
officers serve two months on these Japanese Sampans upposed
to attend fishing around the Hawaiian Islands. I am keeping
this knowledge as a secret, but there is no question about
it, that it's the truth.
An Intelligence report from the 13th Naval District
dated 9 April 1931 reads as follows: "A Mr. Harb B. Kenoe,
7305 -18th Avenue, N. W., Seattle. Wash., whose character
and loyalty have been investigated with entirely favorable
results made the following report to this office: That
from time to time off the Salmon banks he has noticed
parties of Japanese apparently making surveys of the coast-
line, and that about 9 or 10 March, 1931, he noticed
three Japanese ashore at Cattle Point with a. box like in-
strument mounted on a tripod and a large portable camera.
One was giving readings from the instrument and another
was making notations on what apneared to be a piece of draw-
ing paper, secured on a chart board. Upon being hailed and
asked what they were doin they immediately took down their
instrument, got in their boat and departed in the general
direction of Bellingham.
Similar reports of Japanese activities in Latin America
and the West Indies received from a representative of L. E.
Materman Company of New York are as follows: The activities
took place in the latter part of 1927 and the first part
of 1928 and referred particularly to Puerto Barrios and
Barranquilla, Colombia, Progreso, Yucatan, involving the
areas on both sides of the eastern entrance to the Panama
Cana1.
Hayne Ellis

The following is an extract from the letter dated 33

Naval Intelligence. Four Sampans arrived at Manzanillo on
a

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
a Japanese steamer. These boats were small, too well
fitted out for fishing murposes and had no cold storage
apparatus for fish preservation, and apparently did not
have sufficient capacity for any great quantity of fish.
At the stern of each was a brass column used for housing
propellers. A partially covered theodolite was observed
on one of them. Each boat was manned by a crew of five
Japanese who appeared to be of a type superior to that of
fishermen. They claimed to have a fishing concession in
Mexico which later developed not to be a concession, but
merely a permit similar to that given to sportsmen. These
same boats were encountered later on at Mazatlan and Guaymas,
although they had left Man anillo prior to Mr. Armour's
departure they did not arrive at Mazatlan until two days
after Mr. Armour's arrival although the yacht of the latter
has a very slow speed.
The Manager of the Royal Hawaiian Corporation, Hilo
Beach in a letter of 31 December, 1930, to Governor Lawrence
M. Judd, of Hawaii stated as follows: I feel bound to in-
form you that the Government of Japan makes their naval
officers serve two months on these Japanese Sampans supposed
to attend fishing around the Hawaiian Islands. I am keeping
this knowledge as a secret, but there is no question about
it, that it's the truth.
An Intelligence report from the 13th Maval District
dated 9 April 1931 reads as follows: "A Mr. Harb B. Kenoe,
7305 -l8th Avenue, N. W.. Seattle, Wash., whose character
and loyalty have been investigated with entirely favorable
results made the followin report to this office: That
from time to time off the Salmon banks he has noticed
parties of Japanese apparently makin surveys of the coast-
line, and that about 9 or 10 March, 1931, he noticed
three Japanese ashore at Cattle Point with a box like in-
strument mounted on a tripod and a large portable camera.
One was givin: readings from the instrument and another
was making notations on what an eared to be a piece of draw-
ing paper, secu ed on a chart board. Upon being heiled and
asked what they were doing they im ediately took down their
instrument, got in their boat and departed in the general
direction of Bellinsham.
Similar reports of Japanese activities in Latin America
and the est Indies received from a representative of L. E.
Taterman Company of Hew York are as follows: The activities
took place in the latter part of 1927 and the first part
of 1928 and referred particularly to Puerto Barrics and
Barranquilla, Colombia, Progreso, Yuctan, invo ving the
areas on both sides of the eastern entrance to the Panama
Canal.

were presumed to be engaged in pearl fishing, but there
were rumors to the effect that these vessels were en-
gaged in charting and mapping coves, inlo ts and habors
of the west coast of México.
In the above instruction this office is instruct-
ed to report to the Department any reliable information
which may be obtained relative to the truth or falsity
of these rumors.
The first information received by this office ro-
/ILED
lative to these sampans was furnished by Mr. Allison V.
Armour, owner of the Yacht UToWANA which called in this
port on March 28, 1931, with a commission sponsored by
en route to Guaymas and other points in the Gulf of
California

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
California. This ye cht returned to Mazatlán on April
11, 1931, from points along the Gulf of California and
further reported that during the voyage up the west
coast of México these sampans vere encountered at Aca-
pulco, Manzanillo, Mazatlán and Guaymas, and that sus-
picions had been aroused by the fact that no fishing
paraphernalia could be seen aboard these sampas, and
to the fact that they ere truvelin: leisurely up the
coast.
A few cays after 'he depurture of the yacht UTOWANA
an opportunity was had to make an in uiry of a party of
three native fishermen from Puerto Vallarta, Jalisco,
and San Blas, Nayarit, relative to the mov ements of these
sam ans. These fishermen stated that they put into both
the above ports, remaining ap roximately one day in each
port, and that soundings were taken, and that one of the
Japanese on board was drawing maps while in the above
ports, and that photographs of the harbors were taken
from these sampans. Unconfirmed information has been
received to the effect that these sampans also put into
the port of Altata, inuloa.
Discreet inquiry has been made of the Port Captain,
Mazatlán, relative to the business of these sampans in
Mexican waters, and he informed this office to the ef-
fect that the man in charge of this expedition, Naotaro
Kobayashi, was in possession of a concession from the
Moxican Govemment to fish in the waters of the northern
part of the Gulf of Calif ornia, and that when these sam-
pans left this port clearance papers were issued to them
for

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
for the port of Guaymas. He also stated that these
sampans went to Acapulco to pick up Naotaro Kobayashi,
who arrived there by automobile from México City, and
that they were traveling leisurely up the coast to the
fishing crounds at the northern end of the Gulf of Cali-
fornia. Apparently the Port Captain is not under the
impression that these sampans had any other motive than
that of fishing.
This office has no further evidence on hich to
base its suspicions other than that supplied by Mr. Armour,
and the three fishermen, and as the officials do not ap-
pear to be conizant of any motive for these sampans being
in these waters, other than for fishing purposes, it does
not appear that this office can further cubstantiate its
suspicions in this matter.
It should be taken into consi deration, however, that
these four sampans ith approximately l2 men, evidently
sailed from a west coast port of the United States for
Acapulco to meet Mr. Naotaro Kobayashi and take him up
the coast to the northern part of the Gulf of California,
and that thim voyage of over 3,ooo miles was much more
expensive than if Mr. Kobayashi had gone by train from
México City to Guaymas, or some other convenient port
on the Gulf, where these sampans could have met him and
taken him to the fishing grounds.

CONFIDENTIA:
Fort Sam Houston, Texas

SUBJECT: Japanese Marine activities in
the vicinity of Guaymas, Sonora.
Mexico.
TO
Asst. Chief of Staff. -2. War Department,
Washington, D.C.
1. The following statement, dated December 14. 1931, has
been obtained from Royal J. Swift, Apartado l00, Guaymas, Sonore.
Mexico, an employee of the Pierce Oil Company!
"I sent one of my employees aboard the Japanese
fishing boats that are in Guaymas and he says that the
boats have an appearance of anything except fishing.
The ahief of these boats speaks English rather well and
appears to be a very high class man for a fisherman.
These boats are constantly going out to sea up and down
the coast and up until the present date have not been
known to bring in any fish.
"It is my belief as I expressed it to you when you
were here, that these are government men and there seems
to be no doubt among the local people that the first offi-
cer is a Japanese government engineer taking official
notes on this strip of coast. These boats have made sev-
eral visits to Magdalene Bay and have been making soundings
there. They have asked for a grant of land there to estab-
lish a colony but I understand that it was refused."
2. This report is forwarded to follow up reports made recently
by this office on the same subject.
(signed) L. B. LAPHAM.
L. B. Clapham,
Major. General Staff.

(2) Report is quoted:
"Upon my arrival here I immediately sought transpor-
tation to San Luis, some 27 miles a little west of south, on
the Maxican side.
"There is no regular service operating - there being
little or no travel - and I was fortunate in securing trans-
portation in a private car to San Luis.
"Here I found many obstacles in the way of my further
progress: I required 'Form 10 from the Secretariat of Relaciones
Exteriores' from Mexice, the only alternative being to seek this
permission by telegraph and it soon was apparent that I should
not be permitted to proceed south of San Luis under any ciroum-
stances, it having been my intention to go on to Port Isabel.
some 82 miles almost due south of San Luis. This apparent in-
tention of placing obstacles in my way was not without signifi-
cence to me and I made some inquiries regarding Japanese activi-
ties in this territory and learned that a party of 30-odd Japanese
had actually been working to the South of San Luis and between
that place and El Mayor and Port Isabel at the extrome northern
and of the Gulf of California and that they had been there for a
matter of probably three weeks but were no longer in that part
of the country, having departed for some destination unknown to

FAH EASTERN AFFAIRS
Washington, D. C.
EEB 10 1932
16 1932.
Dear Mr. President:
Department of State
February
I trust you will pardon my writing; but during an
experience of twenty-three years in the agricultural machinery here,
dealing constantly with Japanese ranchers, I was gradually 1mpressed
with the constantly inoreasing numbers of Japanese in Mexico, now
amounting to a formidable host as I understand. They are practicall
all hardy, aggressive young men, used to arduous labor and rough
living, and have mostly had more or less military training. They
could almost over night be mobilized into an army of major proportio
that could strike swiftly and deeply across our unprotected Mexican
border, as nearly every Japanese rancher owns his own motor-truck.
Our oil fields are about all along that border. Once in possess
of our oil supply, the invaders could quickly paralyze our air forc
our mobile transportation, and our navy.
I have no doubt but that our War Department has taken these fact
seriously, and I hope they will see that the Mexican border is heavi
protected before an enemy finds an exouse to strike at us without
warning-- as was done to the Chinese. At least I trust that plenty
arms and ammunition will be available at all our main centers of pop
lation along the border, in order that civilians may not be left
helpless in an emergency. Even Los Angeles is only one night by tru
from the Imperial Valley and Mexicali, and most of our oil fields ar
not much further by motor from the Mexican line.
I think an investigation will surprise you at the excessive
numerical strength of the brown race in Mexico, and it is well known
that they carry out the orders of their emperor or die in the attemp
Also, 1f you know Mexicans intimately, Texas and California still
rankle in their breasts not much less than Alsace-Lorraine in the
hearts of Germans. And the insult of a white skin to those socially
discriminated against because of darker color is something that
cannot be atoned for by the white man.
I submit the above in a spirit of patriotic interest, and trust
it may not be taken amiss. I of course do not even mention such id
publicly or at home, as I am not an alarmist, and I am aware of the
dangers of even confidential rumors. But I believe the menace is
real and feasible, with the present unprotected state of our border
Very respectfully yours,
Meston Havtga
(S. Weston Hastings)

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.98)
COPY
11032
offie of the Assistant Chief of staff, 2,
Hoadquarters Eighth Corps Area,
Fort Sam Houston, Texas.
January 18, 1932.
112
Translation of an article entitled "Why Japan Hates the
United States", published in CARTELES, a Havana, Cuba, magazine.
(Editor's note: Owing to the kindness of H. Hama, dis-
tinguished member of the Japanese Colony in Havana, we are able
to offer our readers this juicy chapter of the book "The Next
Japanese-American War" published in Tokyo by General Kiokatsu Sato.
This chapter gives an outline of the causes of Japanese resentment
against our northern neighbors. Since a war between Japan and the
United States will surely affect Cuba to a very large extent, we
have deemed it convenient to familiarize our readers with these
important problems of international politics.)
I do not want to say that the United States is our hypo-
thetical enemy, or use other similar euphemisms. I do affirm
emphatically that the United States is our enemy. We ought to
regard the Yankees as our enemies on account of the numerous in-
justices heaped upon us. And, even though there has never been a
war between us, our people cannot consider that nation (U. S.) as
their friends.
Some Japanese say: "The American Commodore Perry was the
man who put us in touch with the Occident ai the end of the Shoguns
Government. Perry was a benefactor of our nation."
This is a great error. Perry did not come to our land in
search of our friendship, nor did he come to offer us the friend-
ship of his country. According to various documents in the hands
of our Government, Perry came to our country with the purpose of
taking possession of it, but since the Shogun Government did not
offer any resistance, there was no occasion for disturbances, and
he did not have a chance to use his arms against us.
That Perry was a benefactor of our country, and similar
nonsense, are nothing but foolish assertions of unscrupulous his-
torians, who know their history but superficially. The American
Commodore Perry was an enemy of our country, who attempted to take
possession of it by force.
Since the opening of Japan to the outside world we have
experienced a very rapid development and increase in strength,
assuming e prominent position in Oriental affairs. This is a
fact very disagreeable to the United States.
Time and again we have swallowed our resentment caused by
the numerous insulte the Americane have heaped upon us. Due to
considerations of world peace, and the ne cessity of completing our
national forces we have endured very shameful affairs.
1

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
However, we have not suffered these insults patiently, nor have we
submitted to them voluntarily. We are waiting for the time to ( renge
so many insults. We ought not to say that the Yankees are our future
emomies, but admit frankly that they are our present enemies.
Japanese Excluded from Lands and Schools.
Our country is small and narrow, and all the tillable land
is under cultivation. For this reason since the first year of the
era of Meiji, many of our nationals have migrated legally to the West
Coast of the United States, where there were great tracts of land with
out cultivation. The hard working Japenese tilled the soil. For
many years they were engaged in clearing the brush and surmounting
other difficulties. After many sacrifices they succeeded in changing
this unimproved land into gardens.
The Nosth Americans took possession of the lands which
the active, laborious, but unfortunate Japanese, had made productive.
The North Americans took the right of ownership away from them. And
that was not all; the Japanese were forbidden to lease these lands.
All land that was good and cultivable was grabbed by the Americans
as if it wore their own. Is that not a cruelty inflicted on our
emigrants?
And as if that were not enough the North Americans denied
equal social rights to the sons of our nationals. They were denied
public educaticn in the primary schools. Is that not another cruel-
ty against our emigrants?
The United States Does Not Concede Japan an Iimigrant uota.
We endured that kind of treatment at the hands of the
United States because we thought that the time would come when the
American nation would become aware of her injustices. But the North
Americans, always fatuous and arrogant, have not yet become aware
of them. They continue to impose new  justices and inequalities
upon us. For this reason we harbor a great enmity against them. We
only wait for the day when we may have a chance to settle accounts,
and bring about justice by our own hand.
Our country occupies a place among the five most important
nations of the world. We have signed a reciprocity pact with the
United States. It is proper to expect that citizens of equal
countries be treated with equality. The United States has not been
content with injuring our emigrants, but have also injured our ne-
tional dignity. e have had to suffer the cruel treatment given our
emigrants, end in virtue of the so-called Jentlemen's Agreement we
agreed voluntarily to send no more emigrants to the United States.
We have abided by the pact. We have not permitted a single emigrant
with the exception of tourists, students, and bus iness men to go to
the United States. Yet the North American Government was not satis-
fied. In l924 a new Immigration Law was passed which prohibits ab-
solutely the entry of our nationals in Amorican Territory, classify-
ing us as a race not eligible for citizenship, while all European
countries were granted a quota. This is without doubt an insult to
our national honor.
- 2-

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.98)
United Staten Compels Japan to Return Taingtao to China.
Blood is thicker than water. We participated in the Great
War, honoring our Anglo-Japanese Alliance. We sent a division
and a half of troops to Taingtao in the Province of Shantung,
China, and captured that fortified zone from Germany in l914.
China ceded that place to us, according to the clauses of the
Treaty of Peace signed in Paris in l9l9. The United States compelled
us to return it to China in the washington Conference in 1921. Our
delegate to that conference was opposed to the move and refused to
capitulate, but the United States forced us to give it back.
Though the Tsingtao occupation was an obstacle to the
American policy of the Open Door, forcing us to relinquish it was
a signal injustice. In so doing the Americans inflicted an offense
on the souls of those who fell during the fight for the oceupation
of Tsingtao. e have to avenge these souls. The cost does not mat-
ter. It is a sacred obligation to avenge them so that they may be
restored to peace.
The United Statee and the Anglo-Japangse Treaty.
The United 3tates was responsible for the abrogation of the
Anglo-Japanese Alliance. This alliance began in 1902, and protected
the mutual interests of both nations. On the other hand it was the
backbone of our international politics, and enabled us to take an ac-
tive part in international affairs.
All the events that have transpired since then, - the vie-
tories of the Japanese troops in the Russo-Japanese War; the Franco-
Japanese Accord, attained shortly after the War: the Russo-Japanese
Treaty; the participation of Japanese Troops in the allied operations
during the Boxer Rebellion in 1900; the ocoupation of Tsingtao, Shan-
tung; the occupation of severul groups of German islands in the West-
ern Pacific; the Siberian Expedition; the participation in the Treaty
of Peace after the World's ar, all these events are inescapably re-
lated to the Anglo-Japenese lliance.
At the end of the uropean ar, ngland, heavily indebted
to the United States, was receptive to all North merican suggestions.
The United States feared that Japan would opposo their policy of the
Open Doer in China. They reasoned that the greatness of Japan's power
had its origin in the nglo-Japanese Alliance. They forced Great Brit-
ain to cancel this alliance in 1921, when its term expired. So Japan
was completely isolated in the world of diplomacy. In this manner North
American struck a heavy blow at us. Ne detest such measures.
The United States Interferes In Manchuria.
The United Sta es was not satisfied with forcing England to
abandon the Anglo-Japanese Alliance. They lso abrogated a little
later the Ishii-Lansing Agreement. By the terms of this agreement the
United States acceded to recognize the special rights and interests se-
cured by Japan in Manchuria and Mongolia as a result of the wars with
China and Russia.
With the objeet of obtaining raw materials for her industries
and a market for her manufactures, the United States maintains the pol-
1ey of the Open Door in China.
~3。

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
As the recognition of our special rights and interests in Manchuria
was counter to the workings of that policy, the United States forced
the abrogation of the Ishii-Lansing Agreemont at the Washington Con-
ference.
Our sphere of influence in Munchuria and Mongolia is some-
thing for which we have paid an inealculable price. we have to de-
velop the territories of Manchuria and Mongolin in the years to come
to provide subsistence for the Japanese race. The United States, who
had recognized our special rights and interests in Manchuria and Mon-
golia, have now revoked that racognition. This is tantumount to de-
priving the Japanese people by force of its right to subsist. The re-
pudiation of the pact, is one step forward in the evolution of North
American ambitions, and a clear-cut example of the oppression and
tyranny dirested against us.
After the Great r, when the Huropean Powers were juet be-
ginning to recover from the effects of the armed conflict, the United
States suddenly announced the Washington Conferance. They invited
Japan, England, France, and Itely, and proposed the limitation of
naval armaments in the ratios 5-5-3-1.75-1.75. North America imposed
that proportion of forces on the other countries. The United States
and England were able to conserve their superior forces, limiting the
other nations to an inferior percentage. nd later at the recent Naval
Conference in London, we were compelled to limit the auxiliary ships of
our Navy. The sovereignty of a nation is something that cannot be
limited by other nations. The military preperedness of a nation is
one of the things that the sovereign nation decides for herself. Na-
tional Defense and military preparedness are the rights of a severeign
nation, and they re absolute, undeniablc, and non-limitable.
In the ashington Conference in 1921, the United States com-
pelled us to limit our dreadnoughts to 60% of theirs, end just recently
they compelled us to restrict our auxiliary ships.
The United States and the Inferiority of Japan.
In doing that the United  tates has viol ted the sovereignty
of our country. e ought to harbor no resentment against our delegates
that had to resign themselves to the exigencies of the United tates,
but we ought to feel justifiable indignation towards the United tates,
because they forced cn us a naval ratio so unequel and so unjust. Again
the American nation spits on our face. It is our duty to hate the
United States.
There is no need of employing euphemisms or high-sounding
phrases. We ought to speak with frankness and without reserve. Cur
enemy is the United States of North Americal The United States, re-
presentatives of injustice in the world, is our enemy. There is no
neod of hesitation and kind words. I am fully convinced of all I have
said. If there is anybody who does not believe this, let him be con-
vinced.
Let us rush! Let us get ourselves busy! Let us prepare our-
selves for the coming Japanese-American War! We have to punish our
onemy, the United States of North Americal
Literal Translation:
By: B. N. COLON,
Sgt. C.I.P.

CONFIDENTIAL.
Subjest: Information.
To:
Assistant Chief of Staff. G-2.
Eighth Corps Area, Fort Sam Houston, Texas.
1. The information given below is forwarded in confirmation
and amplification of a report sent you from this office early in
Deaember, 193l. This information was obtained frem a souree whioh is
censidered reliable and is believed to be correct.
The report is that in November, 1931. a Japanese warship
amshored off Guaymas, Sonora, and dropped three gaseline launches,
each lmnch having a crew of four or five Japanese, suppesed te be
fishermen. These launohes worked meking soundings of the Gulf of
Lewer California from Guaymas to the north as far as Camp Tornillo,
a fishing villago near the mouth of the Colorade River and about
eighty miles southeast of Yuma, Arizona. These boats worked par-
ticularly from Tiburon Island up to Camp Tornillo, and visited Puerto
Neriega, Puerte Libertad, Puerto de Lobos, Saint George Bay and Adair
Bay. One of these Japanese boats was reported as being at Camp Tor-
nillo en Janmary 1. 1932. It was reported that these Jap boats would
move out into the Gulf and then travel in zig-zag lines back and forth
making soundings, using fish lines, but they caught no fish and never
brought any fish to shore. It was the opinion of the informant that
these Japs were not fishing but making soundings of the depths of the
harbors. It was also reported tha: freight trucks were hauling gaso-
Line from San Luis, Sonora. to Camp Tornillo for these Jap boats.
On Jamary llth, according to a report, a Japanese engineer passed
through San Luis, Sonora, enroute to Baja (Lower) California, with a
letter of recommendation and a pass from the Mexican Minister of
Finance in Mexico City, requesting safe condust for this engineer.
2. My informant stated that he believes the Jspanese will
replace, in Sonera, the Chinese who were run out of that state. He
stated that the Japanese on the Sonora coast have been putting out
propaganda in that area, both to obtain the sympathy of the Mexican
people and to harm the United States.
(signed) WM. NALLE.
e. Nalle.

sinoe about Jammary lst, 193?. He stated that he was informed

February 11, 1932.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE AD JUTANT GENERAL:
Subjeot: Japanese Activities in Mexico.
The Secretary of War directs that a confidential letter
substantially as follows be sent to the Commanding General, 8th
Corps Area, Fort Sam Houston, Texas:
1. Receipt is acknowledged of letter dated
February 2, 1932, to the A. C. of S., 2, 8th Corps
Area, from the A. C. of S., G-2, First Cavalry Division,
Fort Bliss, Texas, forwarded by lst Indorsement from
your headquarters, February 4. 1932 (a-2 091 - Japan,
X 091 - Mexico).
2. The information contained in this letter
concerning the presence of fourteen Japanese engineers
on Jamuary 24, 1932, in the vicinity west of Pearson,
Chimahua, working with level and transit equipment,
and presumably engaged in mapping that strategic area,
adds considerable significance to previous reports of
similar activities of Japanese engineers in the State
of Sonora; also, the report that such activities were
carried on about three years ago in the Sierra Madre
country in the vicinity of Sierra Pacheco (Colonia
Pacheco), Chihuahua, and northward to the United States
border, is of further importance.
3. Confirmation of all the above information
is highly desirable, as is also further information con-
cerning any extension of Japanese activities in the
vicinity of the United States border. It is therefore
requested that you take such steps as are practicable
with the means at your disposal to follow up this matter
and keep this office advised of the results.
ALFRED T. SMITH,
Colonel, General Staff,
A. 0. of S., G2.

2
4
As regards the mmber of Japanese who have recently
entered Mexico through Pacific Coast ports,- a contact of this office,
fairly reliable on other occasions, has reported that on Ootober 8.
1931, 30 Japanese, and on Ootober 22, 1931, 12 Japanese, a11 claiming
to be fishermen, did land in Guaymas, Sonora, and that they brought
in surveying, photographic, and mapping equipment. This same contact
also reported that between Ootober 22, 1931 and Jamuary 28, 1932, a
total of 3l6 Japanese entered Mexico through Pacific Coast ports.
In several conversations which the Aoting M/A has
had recently with Major Mammel J. Solis, a Mexioan aray officer who
served as Military Attache to Japan in 1927, 1928 and 1929, he has
stated that both General Calles and President Ortis Rubio are opposed
UO
to any colonisation schemes of the Japanese in Mexico. He stated that
in 1928 the Japanese Government proposed that if the Mexican Government
would permit the colonisation of Japanese on the Pacific Coast of Mex-
ico, from Topolobampo, Sinaloa, to Acapulco, Guerrero, the Japanese
Government would in return build dry docks, railroads, automobile roads
ete., without cost to Mexico. Major Solis stated that upon his return
to Mexico from Japan, in l929, he was consulted concerning this soheme.
by the Mexican Government, and that upon his recommendation, it was
turned down.

Mexico as laborers  account of the present restrictive immigration
law passed last year.
Robert E. Oummings,

JR
an incident involving the Japanese fishing vessel
DEPT. OF STATT
894.20212/326
KAMINE.
The VERGANA arrived at insenada last evening,
Tbruary 19, 1932, and the KAINE during the night.
This morning the two vesscls were unchored near
ach other. At utout : St or : oo a.., the KAmINE
ran up the Japanese flag. Owin to the fact that
the KAMINE has "san Die go" printed on its stern, as
its home port, those on the VsRGalia assumed that it

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Consulate believes that the incident passed
unnoticed by port authorities and by all others ex-
cepting the members of the crews of the two Japanese
vessels and the VERGANA and one or two seamen on a
vessel engaged in the liquor trade. For this reason
the Consulate hesitates to make an investigation which
might lead to publicity and will await the Department's
instruction in the matter.
The Master of the VERGANA has been cautioned
against taking action or permitting any member of his
crew to take action not authorized by existing United
States laws or regulations.
It is the Consulate's understanding that the name
of the port printed on the stern of any vessel, known
as the home port, is the port where that vessel is
registered by the government whose flag it has the
right to fly. If this is the case, it would appear
to the Consulate that the appropriate authorities at
San Pedro and San Diego might wish to examine into the
right of Japanese fishing vessels to indicate a home
port in the United States.
Respectfully yours,
Wm. A. Smale,
American Consul.

EXCERPTS PROM RECENT CORRESPONDENCE
RECEIVED IN MILITARY INTELLIGENCE DIVISION.
Tijuana, Baja California, Mexico.
Jammary 27, 1932.
" * * * for three months there has been at this place a group of
15 Japanese, and I was much surprised to learn that all of them are
Japanese army officers of high rank, and that three of them are military
engineers, who, from their camp, 'El Real' de Castillo, and from this
place, have made explorations and drawings in a secret manner of the
territory extending from San Diego, California, to Bahia Msgdalena, Mexi-
co, by sea as well as by land. This work was carried out by them dis-
guised as fishermen or other persons, and I am sure that all this was done
with the aid and consent of the Mexican Government.
"Tou may recall, * * * that during the recent World War, there was
located at this same place chosen for the Japanese camp, a German secret
wireless station * * * and * * * the Germans * * * at that time, similar
to the Japanese now here, were disguised as mining prospectors. Although
the Mexican immigration authorities were fully aware that the above-men-
tioned Japanese disembarked without filling out the necessary papers, there
being no immigration office here, nevertheless the Japanese were not molest-
ed at all."
(Excerpt from letter to Secretary of War from Mexican of unknown reli-
ability.)
San Diego, California,
February 1, 1932.
"For the past month there has been considerable activity on the part
of Japanese in this neighborhood, not only in San Diego but at various
points along the Mexican Coast to the South. Just what this means I am
not prepared to say. It is possible that it only means the return of
certain selected reservists to Japan and nothing more. * * * Some days
age a party of about twenty-five (25) Japanese men, all of the better
class, under charge of one of their mumber who made all arrangements and
paid all bills, went by train North. Judicious questioning brought forth
the fact that their destination was the Orient. About the same time e
similar party was gathered in Ensenada, Lower California, and departed
on a north bound coastwise steamer for Los Angeles. As I say these men
are probably reservists being recalled for military duty. Investigation
in and about San Diego, where there are comparatively few Japenese reri-
dents, does not reveal that any of these men came from this vicinity and

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.98)
ONFIDENTLA
I have not yet discovered from what part of this country or of Lower
California they came. On a guess I should say the Imperial Valley
but I do not kmow. In addition to this, scattered information from
points along the Gulf of Lower California seem to indicate that there
is an increased activity among Japanese fishermen at certain points
which the business in which they are concerned does not seem to
justify.
"I have not yet had time or opportunity to check this information
thoroughly but hope to do so in the near future. It would seem absurd
to imagine that Japan was planning any move on this Coast at this time
but * * * you will realize that it is much better to be over-ceutious
than it is te ignore indications of Japanese activity in this neighber-
hood when they crep up. * * *"
(Exoerpt from letter to A.0. of S., G-2, W.D., from thoroughly re-
liable source.)
"I find the Japanese have evacuated. I could not find a trace
of a single one. Just what has become of them, nobody seems to know."
(Excerpt frem report on general conditions in Sinaloa and Sonora -
ceived from a reliable informant of G-2, First Cavalry Division, Fort
Bliss, Texas, and forwarded February 4, 1932.

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
(COPY)
(2657-H-390)
1
Translation of
Artiele appearing in "El Noroeste", Nogales, Sonora, under date of
February 2, 1932.
(de Revistas de Revistas)
WAR BETWEEN JAPAN AND THE UNITED STATES IS INEVITABLE.
By: Lt. Gen. Kio Katsin Kato.
The opinion which Lt. General Kiokatsu Sato, at the present time
in the reserve, expresses herein will, undoubtedly, surprise many persons
who ignore the deep sentiments of the Japanese military party. We are
publishing it exactly as it was transmitted to us, in spite of the impre-
catory violence of some of its passeges, precisely because it very accu-
rately represents the belief of a big majority of the chiefs of the Japan-
ese anmy and navy and of the political men who are its interpreters.
More than a quarter of a century has transpired since the Russian-
Japanese War. The Japanese nation, during this time, has been dreaming
the sweet dreams of peace.
The Japanese race had assumed, with perfect confidence, the mission
of directing the destinies of the Extreme Orient. But now we have lost
our confidence and forgotten our mission. We have pemitted ourselves to
be dragged down and seduoed by the pleasures of the heur and by the ex-
terior signs of a national civilization. We no longer think of the ideas
of our Emperor Meiji nor of the memories left in our ennals by the great
men of his epoch.
During this time, on the other side of the Pacific, the United States
of America have inaugurated a policy of conspiracy and intrigue directed
against the task and mission which concerne us in Asia. The United States
have elaberated a plan which has as its object that they alone occupy all
of China, and they are making rapid strides toward the realization of their
plan.
The first thing whioh they undertook was the expulsion of all of the
Japanese from Chinese territory and by this means, they were able to secure
the good will of the Chinese government. Then, they told themselves that
they must rely upon their military strength in order to obtein control of
the affairs of China. Therefore, they adopted a policy of naval expansion.
Everywhere in China they have established centers of power so that
the course of their ections might have weight. American capital is spread
over the entire nation. without taking into consideration the sphere of
Japanese influence and the rights which the Japanese nation has acquired
and is justly entitled to, their activities continue and they do not stop
CONETOEET

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
CONEIDUNT
before any obstacles that may arise.
Furthermore, our countrymen were el iminated from America. The
Japanese residing on the western coast of th United States were treated
with cruelty and rudeness, and although they have sobbed with hunger and
cold, we have turned a deaf ear to their pleas.
The United States government has wounded the pride of the Japanese
nation. They have taken advantage of its dignity. And, in spite of all,
we pretend to ignore all these things.
Although the United States government has stepped all over the rights
which Japan acquired in Manchuria and in Mongolia, through untold sacrifice
and suffering, we have withheld our enger and our indignation.
We remained completely indifferent when the American government
forced us to renounce our claims to Tsingtae, which our soldiers occupied
after a most diffioult and stremuous battle. They also forced us to eva-
cuate the province of Shantung.
The United States government forced us to denounce the anglo-japanese
allianee, which was the muclous of our international policy end which we
had formed after laborious efforts which lested many years. And, yet, our
nation has not trembled with rage.
Finally, using the Disarmament Conference as an excuse, the Americen
government violated the sovereignty of the Japanese nation. They forced
us to abdicate even the right to defend ourselves. Notwithstending, the
Japanese nation did not protest with loud indignation. Have the Japanese
lost all sensibility? Have they lost their feelings?
If we take a retrospective glance over the history of our race during
the last three thousand years, we will see that our ancestors always placed
honor above everything else. we will see that our ancestors always sacri-
ficed themselves, they exerted themselves and geve their very lives for the
good of their country.
When we look at the Japanese race of today, what is it we see? We
have been insulted in every way possible by a neighboring nation. That
nation has prosecuted us; it has spat our eyes; has thrown mud in our face;
has kicked us; has beaten us; has lashed us and has covered us with blows.
Evidently, we do not feel any of this. The fact that we permit our-
selves to be treated thus, is no longer a sign of patienoe, nor modesty,
nor humility. In truth. it is a disgrace and a humiliation. The Japanese
race shall not bear with meekness, treatment such as this!
We detest the Japanese government that, because of indecision and
weakness, has tolerated such injuries and duses. However, we should detest,
oven with more hatred, the government of the United States.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.97)
CONPTAN
We cannot help but detest and despise those people who, depending
solely on their wealth, have forgotten all about justice and equality.
soorning/the principles of Humanity itself.
We cannot help but detest those people whose words are always
sweet and flattering, while their astions are inhuman and unjust in the
highest degree. There is no pardon for such things. In the name of
moral equality, we should proclaim ourselves agents from heaven whose
work it is to punish the guilty.
If they want to perpetuate the existence of their race, the Japanese
must diffuse themselves and evolve over the entire Asiatic continent.
In order to insure the existenae of our nation, we must pursue a
plan of vigorms economic expansion in the Extreme Orient.
We no longer have room in our country for a population of seventy
million inhabitants. And outside of our demographic sphere, we must elso
develop our commercial and industrial possibilities. We also lack room
for further development here.
Japan's only hope is the Asiatic continent on which our future
depends. Only on it can be insured our very life and the existence of
our race.
However, in that very region on which we must build our hopes and
our future, America is already realizing many vast projects and is pre-
paring to take control of Asia.
Unless we destroy the ambitions of that country end unless we
punish them for their infustice, our souls shall know no peace even
after we have left this earth.
If we take a look backward, we see that we have already made war
with China because of the corean situation. We again fought China
because of the mandchu situation. For the problems thst are of interest
to the existence of our race and our actions as a nation, making abstrac-
tion of our personal conveniences, we have always gone to war to regain
our just rights.
The Japanese nation loves peace. We detest war. However, when we
think of the problems which are so closely connected with our lives and
with the existence and future of our race, we see that we must necesserily
make war.
At the present time we are considering to fix un conditions by means
of diplomatic negotiations. However, circumstances will, undoubtedly,
finally foroe us to make war against America. A war between Japan and the
一3-

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.96)
United States is the inevitable destiny of our nation! The Japanese
nation must convince itself of the truth of this?
When we observe the present conditions of our country, when we
medfate on the conditions of other countries and we think of the future
of our own country, we cannot refrain a feeling of uneasiness.
Rage fills our hearts. We must accept these happenings just as
they are, and impress them firmly in our spirits.
一4一

that I hoped he would be careful.

General Rico about the Rodeo to be held in Nogales next

33
"At Lomas they were greeted by General Rico who
introduced them to a Japanese Naval Captain and some of his
staff. The Japanese Naval Captain was reviewing the troops
and one of his staff was taking moving pictures on a 16 mm.
moving picture camera. There were five Japanese in the party.
After the troops had finished their drill the Japanese were
escorted over the camp, and one of them was taking notes all
the time. Upon their return to Headquarters, Officers Call
was blowm, and the assembled offic ers introduced to the Jap-
anese Captain. He spoke a few words to each one, and was
shown great deference by the Mexican officers. He spoke
both English and Spanish well, and told the Intelligence
Officer that he had been in the World War. When he left,
the Guard was turned out and a call sounded on the bugle.
It is believed that he must have been there in an official
capacity, otherwise the troops would not have been turned
out for him. He was accompanied by the editor of 'm Nor-
oeste', a very pro-Japanese Mexican newspaper, and a local
Japanese from Nogales, Sonora, whose name will be produred
later.

was General Enriquez' purpose to visit the United States

da, and he was desirous of enlistin& the cooperation
of the Embassy to this end.

hclosure No. 2 to despatch No.
from the Embassy at Mexico City.
(C O P Y)
CONFIDENTIAL.
Biographical data of ex-Brigadier General Juan Mérigo,
who at present does not occupy any position in the public
·Administration.
General Juan érigo was born in Mexico City and is
50 years of age. He received his early education in
private and Government schools of this City.
In the year 1907 he entered the lilitary College of
Chapultepec, graduating in 1909 as a Sub-Lieutenant of
Infantry, being assigned (causó alta) to the 2lst In-
fantry Battalion.
In the year 1910, for reasons unknown, he left the
Army, but in l9ll joined the revolutionary movement
headed by Francisco I. adero.
In 1912, he received his discharge, together with a
bounty payment of 200.00 pesos.
In 1913 he joined the revolutionary movement headed
by Venustiano Carranza, with the rank of Captain 1st
Class, of Artillery, rising rapidly until, in August
1914, he was promoted to a Colonel and made Chief of the
Artiilery of the Army of the East, com anded by General
Pablo Gonzalez.
In the year 1915, he rose to the rank of Brigadier
General and organized the Brigade which was known by his
name "Brigada Hérigo". In the year 1917 he left the
command of this Brigade and was made Chief of the De-
partment of Artillery or the Department of War and
Marine.
In the year l920 he was relieved as Chief of the
Department of Artillery, and dropped fron the Army.
(Reports are t the effect that he was dropped because
he opposed the Plan de Agua Prieta and refused to join
the Obregonista movement which overthrew President
Carranza).
General Mérigo has taken part in 91 combats and
during his service in the Army was greatly loved by his
men. He is extremely well posted on military affairs.
In the year 1923 he went to Spain, well recommended by
the Minister of that country in Mexico, in order to take
part in the war against the Moors in Morroco, but it
turned out that he went intending to serve as a General,
to which the Spanish Government would not consent.
General

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
General Mérigo now devotes himself to his private
affairs.
In the Territory of Quintana Roo he held a Chicle
concession; and later became the principal stockholder
in a banana industry, sending enormous shipments of
bananas to the United States from the State of Veracruz.
It was reported at one time that he was a member
of the "Grey Automobile" Band (Banda del Automovil Gris),
a gang of thieves which operated in this Capital in 1915
and 1916.
He was in fact engaged in business in this
Capital, but his connection with this Band was never
proven.
At the present time no one has been able to tell
what ex-General Mérigo's work or business is, or what
he is living on.
B

1678

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL.
( C O P Y )
AMERICAN EMBASSY.
OFFICE OF THE MILITARY ATTACHE,
CITY OF MEXICO.
July 13, 1932.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE AMBASSADOR.
The Japanese Naval Attaché, Captain Kohei Ochi,
called on me on July llth, and also had me as his guest
at luncheon on July 13th.
During our conversation on both days he talked of
things in general, but on both occasions gradual ly led
up to the subject of the arrest of a Japanese named
Hamaguchi, by the federal military authorities in
Sonora.
Captain Ochi stated that Hamaguchi was a Japanese
fisherman, who at one time employed thirty or forty Jap-
anese, but recently due to lack of business, had sent
about ten of these fishermen back to Japan. He said he
knew of no reason why Hamaguchi had been arrested, or
why four of Hamaguchi's fishermen had also been arrested
with him. All of the rrests were made in Manzan illo,
and the Japanese were taken to Guaymas where they were
kept under guard.
Captain Ochi tells me that he has called at both
the Foreign Office, and at Gobernacion, here in Mexico
City, and that neither office admits knowing any reason
why Hamaguchi and his men were arrested, and disclaim
any knowledge of the fact that they were to be deported.
Accordin to Captain Ochi, Hamaguchi claims that
he was arrested because he was accused of makin& sound-
ings in the Gulf of California, along the coast of
Sonora.
He says he has recently received a letter from
amaguchi to ti efect that he will shortly arrive in
Mexico City and explain everythins to the Japanese
Legation.
Captain Ochi stated that he was told by his Japan-
ese contacts in Sonora, whom he admits are not always
reliable, that an American agent in Sonora had paid a
sum of money to the Jefatura Commander in the State of
Sonora to arrest and deport Hamaguchi, - and asked me
if I knew anything concerning the matter. I told him
that the information must be false, for the Unit ed
States Government had no agents working in Mexico; that
I was the only military or naval representative in the
whole Republic.
Captain Ochi evidently had samething on his mind
and was

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
and was trying to sound me out as to our attitude, or
what our knowledge was of Japanese activities in the
northern part of Mexico, - especially in Sonora and the
Gulf of California. He continually asked me what in-
terest we could possibly have in Japanese fishermen in
the area mentioned, and stated that if Japanese fisher-
men were making soundings in the Gulf of California it
was probably because they were trying to locate them-
selves on the map which he, Ochi, had rurnished to
Hamaguchi. These maps Captain Ochi claims to have ob-
tained from Washington.
Captain Ochi also stated that there was a lot of
propaganda going on in Mexico against both Japanese and
Americans, evidently for the purpose of creating ill
feelings between these two countries. He said we should
keep up friendly relations, see more of each other, and
talk things over plainly.
I naturally told him that we knew nothing of the
arrest of Japanese fishermen by the Mexican Government,
in Sonora, and had no interest in the activities of
Japanese fishermen in the Gulf of California.
After he mentioned the propaganda going on in
Mexicc against both Japanese and Americans, I asked him
if he had seen the pamphlet "La Guerra Yankee-Japonesa
y,la Actitud de Mexico" written by ex-General Juan
Merigo. He replied that he had not seen the pamphlet,
but that General Merigo had called upon him at the
Japanese Legation several times asking him if the
Japanese had any objections to his either giving le c-
tures or publishing a pamphlet, - both of which would
be propaganda in favor of the Japanese Government; that
he assured General Merigo that he had no objection to
such propaganda, but inquired what his interest was in
doing this. General Merigo made no definite statement
as to his reasons, but asked Captain Ochi for a loan
of 2, 000 pesos in order to publish the pamphlet. The
latter stated to me that he thought Merigo's sole reason
for desiring to write such a pamphlet was monetary gain;
that naturally he did not advance him any money. Says
he is going to buy one of the pamphlets and read it.
Further questioning of Captain Ochi led me to be-
lieve that he knew more about General Merigo than he
admitted. Time and again during the c ourse of our
conversation he told me of different incidents in
Merigo's life, showing me that he was thoroughly familiar
with his past history and activities.
I know of no reason why Captain Ochi should want
to discuss the above matter with me, unless the Mexican
authorities, at the instigation of General Calles and
Minister Teilez, have been taking some action in the
northwestern part of Mexico against Japanese fishermen
who have been reported to the Embassy and to this office
as making soundings in the Gulf of California, and map-
ping roads, etc. It will be remembered that at the
time this office made a report to the War Department,
March 1st, 1932, Amba sador Clark had a talk with
General Calles concerning the activities of the Japanese
in

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
in northwestern Mexico, and the latter then stated that
he knew nothing of such activities, but that if they
were being carried on he would put a stop to it. It
might be that the arrest of Hamaguchi is due to the
Mexican Government taking some action in this case.
Robert E. Cummings,
Captain,Infantry, DOL.
ActingMilitary Attache.
C/p

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AAgust 3, 1932.
There are attached three papers from
M.I.D. and copy of a despatch from the
American Consulate at Guaymas, Mexico,
relating to alleged Japanese activities in
Mexico.
The first enclosure from M.I.D. has
already been circulated in the Division
and discusses the arrest by Mexican authorities
of a certain Japanese fisherman Hamaguchi,
who may have been making soundings in the
Gulf of California for military purposes.
The second enclosure comments about a
Cuadalupe y Calre Japanese dentist who "is
known to be making maps of certain landing
fields". The third enclosure reports the
seizure of a Japanese fishing smack "Kumano"
in the Gulf of California by order of the
Mexican Secretary of War.
The Consul's despatch reports the
seizure of the fishing smack "Kumano" with
the Japanese crew of five including the above
mentioned Hamaguchi. He reports that there
are many rumors to the effect that they were
engaged in charting the coasts of the Gulf
of California for the use of the Japanese
Navy in case of war with the United States.
The

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2 -
The Consul, however, has been unable to
discover any real evidence in support of
suoh rumors. The rumors the Consul states
are not entirely reasonable since detailed
navigation charts and books of the Gulf
of California are issued by the Govern-
ment of the United States and are avail-
able at small cost. The Japanese Govern-
ment could probably obtain permission to
take soundings of the Gulf and the
Japanese residents of that area have un-
doubtedly already submitted information
for the use of the Japanese Navy. The
seized fishing boat is apparently one of
six or seven that were brought from Japan
about ten months ago on a vessel of the
N.Y.K. line. The local immigration official
stated that all of these Japanese entered

1. The Japanese Naval Attache, Captain Kohei Ochi,
called on me on July llth, and also had me as his guest at
luncheon on July 13th.
2. During our conversations on both days he talked
of things in general, but on both oocasions gradually led up
to the subject of the arrest of a Japanese named Hemaguchi,
by the federal military authorities in Sonora.
0:
3. Captain Ochi stated that Hamaguchi was a Japanese
01
fisherman, who at one time employed thirty or forty Japanese,
7
but recently due to lack of business, had sent about ten of
these fishenaen back to Japan. He said he knew of no reason
why Hamaguchi had been arrested, or why four of Hamaguchi's
fishermen had also been arrested with him. All the arrests
were made in Mansanillo, and the Japanese were taken to Guaymas
where they were kept under guard.
4. Captain Ochi tells me that he has called at both
the Foreign Office, and at Gobernacion, here in Mexico City,
and that neither office admits kmowing any reason why Hamaguchi
and his men were arrested, and disclaim any knowledge of the
fact that they were to be deported..
5. According to Captain Ochi, Hamaguchi claims that
he was arrested because he was accused of making soundings in
the Gulf of California, along the coast of Sonora. He says
he has recently received a letter from Hemaguchi to the effect
that he will shortly arrive in Mexico City and explain every-
PILED
thing to the Japanese Legation.

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)

6. Captain Ochi stated that he was told by his
Japanese contacts in Sonora, whom he admits are not always
reliable, that an merican agent in Sonora had paid a sum of
money to the Jefatura Commander in the State of Sonora to
arrest and deport Hamaguchi, - and asked me if I kmew anything
concerning the matter. I told him that the information must
be false, for the United States Government had no agents work-
ing in Mexice, except Consular and Diplomatic officers; that
I was the only military or naval representative in the whole
Republic.
7. Captain Ochi evidently had something on his mind
and was trying to sound me out as to our attitude, or what
our knowledge was of Japanese activities in the northern part
of Mexico, - especially in Sonora and the Gulf of California.
He contimmally asked me what interest we could possibly have
in Japanese fishermen in the area mentioned, and stated that
if Japanese fishermen were making soundings in the Gulf of
California it was probably vecause they were trying to locate
themselves on the map which he, Ochi, had furnished to Hama-
guchi. These maps Captain Ochi claims to have obtaimnd from
Washington.
8. Captain Ochi also stated that there was a lot of
propaganda going on in Maxico against both Japanese and Ameri-
cans, evidently for the purpose of creating ill feelings
between these two countries. He said we should keep up
friendly relations, see more of each other, and talk things
over plainly.
9. I naturally told him that we kmew nothing of the
arrest of Japenese fishermen by the Mexicen Govermment, in
Sonora, and had no interest in the activities of Japanese
fishermen in the Gulf of California.
10. fter he mentioned the propaganda going on. in
Mexico against both Japanese and Americans, I asked him if he
had seen the pamphlet "La Guerra Yankee-Japonesa y la Actitud
de Mexico" written by ex-General Juan Merigo. He replied that
he had not seen the pamphlet, but that General Merigo had c:lled
upon him at the Japanese Legation several times asking him if
the Japanese had any objections to his either giving lectures
or publishing a pamphlet, - both of which would be propaganda
in favor of the Japanese Government; that he assured General
Merigo that he had no objection to such propaganda, but in-
quired what his interest was in doing this. General Merigo
made no definite statement as to his reasons, but asked Captain
Ochi for a loan of 2,000 pesos in order to publish the pam-
phlet. The latter stated to me that he thought Merigo's sole
reason for desiring to write such a pamphlet was monetary gain;
that naturally he did not advance him any money. Says he is
going to buy one of the pamphlets and read it.

It was reported on June 13, 1932, that on authority of the Mexican
War Department a party of soldiers from Guaymas took into custody a
Japanese fishing smack at Puerto Lobos. The boat, reported as the
Kayamat was one of seven which arrived last year. Details were surpressed

(Excerpt from letter to G-2, 8th Corps Area, from Commanding Officer,
25th Infantry, Fort Huachuca, Arizona, June 13, 1932.)
A reliable informant who travels over Chihuahua and Sonor recently
returned from a trip in these states and furnished the following informa-
tion which he obtained from a reliable American living at and employed
by the West Mexican Mines Lt'd at Guadalupe y Calvo, Chihuahua.
A Japanese dentist who lives at Guadalupe y Calvo is believed to
be gathering data for the Japanese government. This Jap is well educated
along engineering lines as well as being a dentist. He is known to be
making maps of certain landing fields between Guadalupe y Calvo and
Mazatlan, and it is believed that he is making maps in genersl of the
territory in that vicinity. He sometimes leaves Guadalupe y Calvo and
visits the surrounding villages for periods of a month or more at a time.
There is never enough dentistry work in this territory to keep this man
employed except for a fraction of the time. During the height of the
Sino-Japanese trouble in the early part of 1932 this Jap was very nation-
alistic and is reported to have lined up several (three or four) hundred
Mexican men for employment as laborers in Japan and Manchuria should he
call for them. He readily secured many applicants for the trip on offer-
ing to pay them $3 gold per day.
(Excerpt from letter to G-2, 8th Corps Area, from First Cavalry Division,
ort B1iss, Texas, June 11, 1932; 2/2657-G-657/82.)
