THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to report that a small Japanese
C
fishing boat, probably under 25 feet in length, the
6
M/S KuMANO NO. 5, which was registered at Guaymas under
the Mexican flag not long ago, with a Japanese crew of
five, viz., Kajiro Hamaguchi, Bantaro Amano, Kiyoji
Hamaguchi, Rinosuke Kavanchi, and Fudekichi Tanaka, has
been detained by the local Federal authorities, together
with its crew, after having been brought from waters df
the Gulf of California near the mouth of the Colorado
River, under orders of the Central Government.
The bringing of this boat with its crew under the
custody of two officers of the Mexican Transport
PROGRESO, on June 7, 1932, and its detention to date,
has been clothed in considerable mystery, from the view-
Confidential
point of local residents and press. In fact, rumors
have been spread at random that this vessel and its orew
have been secretly engaged in oharting the coasts of the
Gulf of California for the specific and exclusive use of
the
File
①

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the Japanese Navy in case of war with the United States.
The consulate, however, has been unable to dis-
cover any real evidence or practical theory which would
justify any such assumption. This belief is expressed,
of course, apart from the oft asserted opinion heard
from many quarters to the effect that each Japanese
subject in foreign countries first serves his country
as a virtual spy and afterwards attends to his personal
interests. Likewise, strictly technical questions whioh
frequently are heard, such as the possible use of the
Gulf of California, with its numerous bays, coves,
islands, access to the United States by land, sea, and
river, the relative narrowness of the entrance to the
Gulf and the consequent possibility of proteoting it
by mines, et cetera, by the Japanese Navy, specially
by its submarines, as a base, are, of course, not with-
in the competenoy of the writer to discuss herein.
Nevertheless, in the belief of the writer, the
theory put forth locally which is stated above, in all
probability is not entirely reasonable since (1) de-
tailed navigation charts and books of the Gulf of
California, issued by the Government of the United
States, are available at small cost, (2) the Japanese
Government could, doubtlessly, obtain special permission
from the Mexioan Government, under some pretext allowed
by international usage, to take soundings and make
charts of the Gulf of California, and (3) the relatively
large number of Japanese now residing in Lower Calif-
ornia , specially along the seacoast, most of whom
are engaged in fishing or allied industries, undoubtedly
have

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
3-
have already submitted data for the use of the Japanese
Navy.
Local residents state that the above-named Japanese
fishermen are not fishermen inasmuch as they do not aot
as fishermen, as they are known here; that their general
appearance as regards type is not of fishermen but of
educated Japanese; that their hands do not indicate them
to be manual workers; that they have special nautioal
instruments; that they have been seen take soundings of
the bays and coves of the Gulf; et cetera. Nothing that
this office has been able to asoertain has borne out
these statements, However.
It is the understanding of this office that the
above-named fishing boat is one of six or seven that
were brought from Japan to Manzanillo, Colima, Mexico,
about ten months ago on a vessel of the Nippon Yusen
Kaisha Line and that all of these fishing boats came
coasting to Guaymas.
It seems however, that the KUMANO
NO. 5 is the only one now in these waters.
The ex-
pressed object of these boats coming to Mexican waters
was to fish and upon the ocourrence of the incidents in
the Far East, there appeared to be no limit to
the rumors
heard here connecting them to those incidents and the
United States. Moreover, at least one person claiming
to be an American is known to have come to Guaymas and
visited the consulate at that time, hinting, according
to Consul Voetter with whom he talked, that he came here
to investigate the matter.
The undersigned officer, however, was informed by
the local immigration official that every one of the
Japanese named above were legally immigrated into the
country

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
oountry at Manzanillo and that, although the
Seoretarfa de Gobernación had ordered the detention of
these Japanese and their boat when they were first
brought to Guaymas, he had subsequently been ordered
to have nothing more to do with the matter.
General Mariano Garay, in command of the troops
stationed at Guaymas and its vicinity, confidentially
informed the writer that these Japanese were being
detained at Guaymas, with liberty to roam in the city,
while they were being investigated in connection with
smuggling to and from the United States via the
Colorado River. He added that they stated that they
were engaged in fishing in the Gulf, but that they
could show no fishing permits mor could they explain
their whereabouts or activities during the two months
prior to their virtual arrest. Incidentally, he added
that it was his understanding that action had originally
been taken by the looal Federal authorities in the matter
upon orders from the Central Government which, in turn,
had been at the instance of the United States Government,
since, in the eventuality of war between the two powers,
this government would take the side of the United States.
General Garay branded the rumors referred to'above as being
entirely without foundation, so far as he was aware.
Respectfully yours,
A. F. Yepis,
American Vice Consul.

SIR:
I have the honor to report an incident which occurred
last night, August 17th, at the weekly Rotary Club dinner.
The reporting officer, who is a member of the local Rotary
Club and who has been on the most friendly terms with all
of the members of it, arrived at the meeting last evening
to find that a recently elected member, Antonio Y. Kurachi,
had distributed a number of pamphlets among tho se present
entitled "The Yankee-Japanese War and the Attitude of
Mexico" (La Guerra Yankee-Japonesa y la Actitud de México).
The pamphlet is nothing more or less than a harsh attack
upon the United States. One copy is being sent herewith to
the Department, the Eabassy und the Consulate General in
México City.
At the proper moment the writer called the attention
of the President, and incidentally the Club as a whole, to
the fact that the Constitution of the Rotary International
prohibits clubs beo ming involved in political questions,
which could well be interpreted to preclude the use of the
Club for the spreading of political propaganda. The re-
marks were made in Spanish and a transcript of them, as
closely

clause of the Constitution of the Rotary Club

political affairs, either local or internetional,
and from which we may infer that the Club should
not be used as a medium of dissemination of
political propaganda, especi lly when it severely
attacks the government of any of the members.
I have here a copy of a pamphlet which I see
has been distri buted among those present tonight
by one of our most recent members. I will not
discuss its contents except to say that it is a
harsh and unjustifiable attack on the country I
represent, cnd this Club is no place ror such

ficially re juest thet a letter be sent to the
member who distributed these pamphlets, oal line
his attention to the provision of the Constitution
prohibiting such ection, and instructing him to
abstain from it in the future".

Translation. ("Varguardia, August 18, 1932).
INCIDENT AT TE ROTTAN SUT PLR OF LAST NIGHT,
Occassioned by the distribution of a pumphlet that contains
hard expressions regarding the nei hboring country of North
America.
A few moments berore the kotary supper which perioaically
occurs on Wednesdays in the "Salón kotario" of the Hotel
Progreso, there occurred an incident of a strongly dis-
agreeable tone, that surely and on account of its nature
being related directly to the sentiment of patriotism innate
in all men, will occasion the protagonists to be somewhat
distant.
A representative of this pper found himself near the
Hotel Progrese when the incident occurred and in this marner
promptly obtained the data necessary to formulate the notice
which is concisely as follows:
Mr. Antonio Kurachi, well-known member of the Japanese
colony and member also of the Rotary club, distriouted
among the persons that were in the salon where & few
minutes afterwards the supner took place, copies of a
pamphlet that eulogize the international policy of Japan
and in turn made harsh critici sm of North Anerica (United
States). The editor of this condensed work being ex-General
Mérigo, very well known in México. The Aaerican Consul,
Mr. Shaw, was in the rotary salon and a copy of the pam hlet
promptly fell into his hands, or which in abundance he found
impressed the commercial s tamp of r. Kurachi.
Immediately the American Consul, with evidences of firm
opposition, took steps to the end that an ample clarifica-
tion of the case be made, as there exist two powerful
motives which obliged him to do it, first. the respect
for his country within an interne tional institution such
as is the Rotary Club, and second, the respect due his
person.
We are keeping this matter pending in order to inform
(publi) to a greater extent in case it becomes necessary.

Army who just arrived in this metropolis proceeding from
Los Angeles, California, where he went accompanying the
Olympic team of his country, has manifested his belief to
the effect that the present political régime of North
Amerioa will shortly disappear, by virtue of the faot that
many weeks will not pass until two of the most powerful
oriental forces will dedicate themselves to invading the
territory of the neighboring country. The high military
chief in question explained, referring to Japan and to
Russia as those whioh made it sure that briefly they would
have to unite in an explicable eagerness to dominate the
colossus of the north. Japan, because its people have
not yet thrown off the hatred of the Yankee which on no
few occasions has received grave injuries; and Russia,
by virtue of the resentment which it holds towards the
Government of the American Union for denying it the recogni-
tion of its present governmental régime.
The person interviewed by us continued saying that
during his short stay in the neighboring country he became
aware that the administ ration of President Hoover was going
through at the present time a dangerous crisis by virtue
of the faot that many of his principal collaborators are
soon to present the resigna tions of their portfolios,
foreseeing the imminence of a conflagration and their
intention of not seeing themselves involved in it.
Further, the aforesaid military chief was able to
observe that among the major part of the working popula-
tion of the United States there exists a very marked dis-
content with the acts of the Government due to the
procedure whioh it has just used with the veterans of the
war, and in accordance with the foregoing it is sure that
in case of an armed international confliot the administra-
tion of the neighboring country would not count but with a
minimum part of its present army, as each soldier would
resist remaining afterwards in the greatest abandonment,
as has happened to the ex-combatants of the Great Wat.
Suoh

-2-
Such situation of the American Union is considered by
Russia and Japan as the easiest of being taken advantage
of for their ideas of expansion and, to judge from the
notices regarding the matter, such a thing will happen in
a very short t ime.
The Corresponaant."

officers."

 JAPANESE DOCTOR SHALL VISIT CAJEME
Dr. Kinta AraI, the Japanese orator
(conferencista), who is traveling through the
Republic as a propagandist for his country, is
about to visit Ciudad Obregón. The professional
in question is proposed to deliver a speech here
on matters concerning his country, in an effort to
picture his country in its industrial aspect and
as a people of a solid culture.
At the same time that Dr. AraI comes to bring
out the qualities of the Japanese Empire, he
illustrates his talks with moving pictures whioh
also depict the tenacious industry of the
oriental people under reference.
The doctor shall probably hold only one
conference here. He is coming from Sinaloa where
he is expected on the 18th instant.
The presentation of Dr. AraI in Ciudad
Obregón probably will be towards the end of the
current mon th.

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
FFORTS TO EXTEND MARKET FOR JAPANESE GOODS
Reference is made to a mimeographed instruction from
the American Consul General, Mexico City, dated November
10, 1933, Circular No. 42, which requested reports on
efforts to extend the market for Japanese goods in the
Nogales consular district, and to the consulate's report
on the matter dated December 19, 1933. At that time the
consulate reported entirely in the negative to the specific
questions in the circular in question, namely (1) Is there
an increasing consumption of Japanese merchandise and to
what extent; (2) Name classes of Japanese merchandise
consumed; (3) Is there any concerted effort on the part
of the Japanese to promote sales of Japanese merchandise
in the distriot? and (4) What is the attitude of the
merchants in general towards Japanese merchandise?
The reply to questions (1), (2) and (4) can still
be negative. The volume of Japanese merchandise sold in
the Nogales consular district is very small and the con-
sulate has seen no evidence of any increase. Although
some interest in Japan and its produc ts seems to have
been aroused by efforts to extend the market for Japanese
goods which forms the sub ject of this report, the general
attitude of local merchants can still be stated as indif-
ferent. They have no prejudice against such products, but
apparently no great interest in them.
Question No. 3 of the circular cannot, however, now
be answered in the negative. Since the beginning of the
year Dr. Kinta Arai, Profesor de la Facultad de Filosofia y
Letras de la Universidad Autonoma de Mexico, has been
touring

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
touring through the district delivering a series of
lectures concerning Japan. He delivered one leoture in
Hermosillo on January 4, 1934, and two in Nogales on
Januery 9th and 10th, 1934. The consulate has not been
able to prcure details of his itinerary, but he has
doubtless leotured elsewhere in the district. It is noted
that his programs at Hermosillo and Nogales were identical
and it seems probable that he has it arranged so that what
he wishes to say may be made to cover either one or two
lectures. In regard to the lecture in Hermosillo the
consulate received a report from an American citizen
resident in that city to the effect that he felt that
the lecture was "rather antagonistic to the United States,
altho there was no direct mention of the United States."
The consulate found nothing in the Nogales lectures to
justify that statement and believes either that he modified
the lecture for the border city, or that the American
citizen in question was over-zealous in his patriotism.
However, the object of the lectures can not be doubted.
There was no direct appeal for the expansion in the sale
of Japanese goods, but while the lectures reviewed all
phases of Japanese life, there was an undoubted emphasis
on the commercial side.
The lectures in Nogales were very well attended.
Advanoe notices and the invitations to attend, which were
distributed by the local Japanese community, did not refer
to the commercial aspects of the lectures and the audience
was naturally mixed. Whether the leotures attained their
objeot of arousing commercial interest remains, for the
present

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
present at least, an open question.
It might be added that the lectures were followed
up by the publication of an artiole headed "Intercambio
Comeroial con el Japon" in "El Noreste" for January
10, 1934. "El Noreste" is the only newspaper published
in Nogales, Sonora. A translation of the article in
question is enclosed.
Encl osure:

Translation of Article from "El Noreste,"
January 10, 1934)
Commercial Exchange with Japan
Mexico has the opportunity of increasing its
commercial relations with Japan, what with our commodities
such as cotton, henequin, chicle, raw tobacco, candle
wax, etc., while in exchange we can purchase from the
sons of the eastern sun some menufactured articles which
are not made in Mexico, such as lamps and eleotrical
appliences, sporting goods, silks, etc., with the
added advantage that by trading with Japan we can buy
them cheaper than in any other country.
The Department of Foreign Commerce of the Secretaria
de Economia of Mexico, D. F. furnished this information,
stating that the "NYk" Steamship Company is now ready to
grant a ∞ nsiderable discount in its carrying charges
to Mexican ports from Japanese ports, where a decided
advant age is given the company in the volume of the
ocean going trade.
In this manner our products can be sent to Japan
at a much cheaper rate, and in turn our articles can be
sold cheaper where a demand exists for them.

I have the honor to report to the Department that
on February 2nd and 3, 1934, a Japanese propagandist
by the name of Dr. Kinta Arai, claiming to be a
professor of philosophy in the University of Mexico,
gave a series of free lectures in Ciudad Juarez, spon-
sored by the local Japanese colonies in Ciudad Juarez
and El Paso. Printed notices of the lectures were
distributed both in El Paso and Juarez among the Mex-
MAR
ican people.
FILED
八
Believing that Dr. Arai's lectures were undoubted-
2
2
ly in the interest of Japanese propaganda and probably
191
3
initiated by the Imperial Japanese Government, it wast
considered necessary thet someone in the confidence of
the Consulate be detailed to attend, with a view to
ascertaining the kind of propaganda this man was dis-
tributing in Mexico.
I conferred with the Military Intelligence Officer
at

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
at Fort Bliss, and it was arranged that one of his under-
cover men would be detailed to attend all of the lec-
tures and make a thorough report thereon. A young man
by the name of Smith, thoroughly conversant in Spanish
and well educated, was detailed to the assignment, and
1/
I enclose herewith copy of his report made to Major
Lacey, Post Intelligence Officer at Fort Bliss, Texas.
I am informed that Dr. Kinta Arai has been lec-
turing throughout Mexico for several months and that
this is his second visit to the border, he having been
at Nogales, Sonora, some time ago, where he gave lec-
tures similar to those given in Juarez. I understand
that Dr. Arai is now in Chihuahua City and from there
will r turn to the interior of Mexico where he is slated
to give lectures and exhibit his motion pictures, un-
questionably prepared for Japanese propaganda.
After conversing with a number of Mexicans who
attended the lectures, I am of the opinion that Dr.
Arai is considered as a Japanese propagendist and that
while his talk was interesting, it was, nevertheless,
accepted by the Mexican people as propaganda in the
interest of Japan with a view to winning the good-
will of Mexico in the event of var at some time in the
future.
The report of the under cover man is very detailed
and, I believe, very accurate.

Dr. Kinta Arai - Jananese.
Feb. 2, 1934. Dr. Kinta Arai's lecture was started
in the "Salon de Novedades" in Juarez at 8:00 PM by his

duration, and was embellished by the usual amount of
"bull" so often encountered in the Spanish language,
about friendship.
Dr. Arai opened his address by stating, very casu-
ally, that he was a professor of ph losophy in the
University of Mexico. This statement was never men-
tioned agein. He stated that his purmose in the lecture
was solely in the interest of peace end understanding;
........ "Understanding and knorledge of each other is
the besis of amiability and friendship between both
nations and individuals."....... This theme was very
closely followed in the entire address, nor could I
discern from his talk any other motive than the above
stated. His talk was principelly on the past of Jepan
and a statement that .... "in the past Japan has fought
because she had to fight to preserve herself.".... He

and she will win because the greatness and glory of
Japan is due to the unity and equclity amons her different
classes and her internal orgenization."....."Japan is
very eificient at war but she vants peace.".... He
favors an international cuirence to promote better un-
derstandin; between nations.
This Jap is a lettered and cultured man who has

He speaks the Hexicen language simply and perfectly and

CONFIDENTIAL
From:
Commandant.
To:
Director of Naval Intelligence.
Subject:
Clippings re Japanese Activities.
Enclosure:
(A) Two sheets of clippings re Japanese Activities.
(B) Clippings re Kinta Arai.
1.
Enclosure (A) is forwarded as being of possible interest
to the Director.
2.
Enclosure (B) is forwarded as being of probable interest
to the Director and possibly to the State Department (copy inclosed).
Professor Arai had previously held three meetings in Tia Juana, Mexico,
the latter part of January; these were covered by an agent from this
District. The meetings were attended by about one hundred and twenty-five
Japanese, mostly ranchers from Lower California, and some seventy-five
Mexicans. The subject of discussion, in general, was Japanese colonization
of Lower California. It appears that Professor Arai holds the same
position of official propagandist for his country in Mexico as Professor
Ken Nakazawa holds in the Los Angeles section. It is believed that the
statement that there are now more than three hundred thousand Japanese in
Mexico is a mis-print.
3.
This propaganda of Professor Arai's may be a part of a
general plan throughout Mexico of a Japanese attempt to regain their recent-
ly weakened friendship with the Mexicans, which they had so laboriously
built up since 1920.
4.
Should more information be desired by the Director on the
sub ject of Professor Arai, it will be obtained.
J. H. EVERSON,
By direction.

MAR 3-134
SIR:
I have the honor to report that on the afternoon
and evening of January 24, and on the evenin: of Janu-
3
ry 25, 1934, there were held in the cinema theatre
52
at Ensenada under the auspices of the Japanese ssoci-
Arai, described in the printed invitations as Professor
of the School of Philosophy and Letters of the Univer-
sity of Mexico. The Consulate has not reported upon
Dr. Arai's lectures earlier because it wished in the
most casual way to ascertain the local reactions to
FILED
1
his speeches.
19
3
During his lectures, one of which I attended and 
others which were attenaed by members of the staff of
the Consulate, as invited uests, Dr. rai explained
that he had been in the Japanese diplomatic service
for many years previous to coming to Mexico and that
he had been a resident of Mexico for many years. He
speaks Spanish fluently.
In

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In addition to his public addresses in Ensenada,
Dr. Arai lectured in Tijuana a few days earlier and it
is reported delivered addresses elsewhere in Mexico
during the same journey.
. The lectures, both in Ti-
juana and Ensenada, were well attended. Although
many of those who assembled were Japanese, those in
charge carefully reserved room for so.many of the
local officials and the better class of residents as
sought entrance.
Dr. Arai explained that his purposes in addressing
the public in Baja California were three. (1). To en-
deavor to bring about a better feeling between Japanese
and Mexican races. (2). To overcome or to refute
certain false reports which had been circulated recent-
1y, and (3), to furnish information about the develop-
ment and progress which had been made in Japan during
modern times.
The lecturer had little pleasant to say about
modern diplomacy. He stated that when diplomats did
not know the languages of the countries to which they
were accredited to it was not possible for them to
understand the inner feelings of the nationals in
those countries, and that, not understanding these
feelings, they could not properly interpret the actions
of those nationals. He deplored the fact that so many
books had been written about Japanese by travelers
who stayed only between boats. He added that true
progress in effecting racial friendship could best be
made by individuals who spoke a mutual language
fluently and who, therefore, might be expected to
understand each other well.
Dr.

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Dr. Arai stated that newspapers had carried the
report that his journey to Baja California was motivated
by the fact that war between the United States and
Japan was inevitable and that he was endeavoring to
obtain the sympathy of Mexico in order that Mexico
should support Japan in the conflict. The lecturer
described such a report as absurd and false and that
there was no basis for any assumption that there
should be a conflict between the United States and
Japan. It is interesting to note, in this connection,
that no one with whom I have spoken has observed in
any newspaper reference to Dr. rai in the sense he
suggests, and one or two persons in casual conversa-
tions have expressed the opinion that the speaker was
but anticipating such an observation in the press.
The other reputedly baseless rumor which Dr. Arai
discussed was that there were in Baja California
Japanese spies interested in obtaining photographs
or information for the use of their government. This
also he termed as absurd and suggested that there was
no reason whatsoever for such espionage. He suggested
that the foundation for any such rumors undoubtedly
was the fact that there is a considerable number of
Japanese in Baja California, many of whom speak only
their own language and can not express themselves
well. Such persons, he asserted, might well be con-
sidered mysterious by those who could not intelli-
gently question them.
In treating of the progress which has been made
in Japan during modern times, the lecturer employed
a series of motion picture projections. He dwelt
upon

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
upon the educational, artistic, and commercial develop-
ments but the grand finale of the first evening's lec-
ture was the tremendous progress and the efficiency of
the Japanese navy and that of the second evening's
lecture was the splendid condition and strength of the
Japanese army.
The lecturer endeavored to show that Japan had
never provoked a war; that in its war with China it
had surmounted victoriously tremendous odds; and that
ten years later it had been victorious over even
greater odds in its war with Russia. He stated that
Japan would never fight save in the face of aggression
or to protect its national integrity. He clearly wished
to leave with his listeners the idea that in view of
Japan's victories over Chine and Russia, in the face
of tremendous obstacles, it now, with the great strides
which the nation has made in the last decade, might
surmount even greater odds.
Candidly, my opinion was that Dr. Arai's attitude
in his plea for peace was somewhat militant. s one
listener observed, he seemed to convey the idea that
Japan would have peace with iexico even though it
were necessary to fight for it. His speech has been
mentioned at a number of social atherings at which
the writer was present and all of my conversations
have been most casual. I have concluded that he ac-
complished little in Ensenada.
Those who may have
been moved favorably were moved but mildly; but those
who were influenced adversely have expressed them-
selves most bitterly. One of the higher ranking
federal officials in Ensenada stated that he intended
at
●

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.96)
-5 -
at the earliest opportunity to deliver a public address
on the subject: "What Dr. Arai Did Not Say."
I am enclosing with the original of this despatch,
newspaper clippings from the LA OPINION of Los Angeles,
California, and from EL HISPANO-AMERICANO and LA
FRONTERA of Tijuana, Baja California. These all refer
to his addresses in Tijuana.
Respectfully yours,
maSmar
Wm.C.male,
merican consul.

Lower California, West Coast - Japanese Activities, etc.
1.The following information is a summary supplied by an informant
who is eonsidered very trustweorthy and observant. This information, just
received by the Distriet, 1s believed to be reliable up to 9 April l934, when
this partieular survey was completed. The information will be again checked
by an erpedition whieh is about to start (see Commandant's confidential letter
N11/0r-33 (A3-sa), dated 31 May 1954).
(a) Location of Japanese Aotivities:
Meme were found north of Blanoo Point.
No. of Japanese
Blanco Point

about 5

(b) Japanese Boats:
Only two Tuna Boats were seen at a distance. Most of the
fishing craft observed were mall Halibut Boats, in very sood
condition and under Mexican registry. ▲ total of eight were seen
but no information could be obtained. The crews refused to talk.
The majority of the Japanese appeared to be of a very intelligent
type.
(c) Scammon Lagoon:
The center of activity on the coast of Lower California
appears to be located on this Lagoon. (Opposite Cedros Island).
Nothing of particular interest was seen at any of the other
activities. ▲ very good landing, showing evidence of considerable
activity, is located in a bight at the north side of the Lagoon,
about fifteen miles from its mouth. ▲ channel with six to ten
fathoms of water extends from a point bearing north from the
entrance and about three miles off shore, into the anchorage.
There is sufficient room for fifty destroyers and unlimited room
for small eraft and planes. On the shore were seen signs of
considerable cement work. There is a well constructed landing
CONPIDENTIAL
field at a mint two miles southwest from Ojo de Leebre and
ergido Peak.

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.97)
NEIDENTIAL
(d) In the survey along the west coast and as far as La Pas,
the attitude of the matives seems to be decidedly
pro-Japamese. The infomant posed as a Canadian and was
takem into their confidence many times. Apparently
Japamese propaganda is very strong. The most prevalent
idea seems to be that Mapan is going to help Mexico in
her development and aid her in regaining the states that
the U.S.A. "stole from her". The Army and Navy officers
with whom the informant became confidential expressed
the hope that Japan would deelare war on the United
States. Some of these were very bitter. Out of
fifty-eight officials of the Mexican Government whom the
informant contacted only six were not pro-Japanese.

Sir:
There is inolosed a copy of a report, dated June 8,
1954, from the office of the Chief Patrol Inspector,
Tuseon, Arizona,to the Department of Labor, in whioh it
is stated that, ascording to information coming from very
reliable sources, "anti-American propaganda is being
spread by Japanese Consuls stationed along the West Coast
of Mexioo".
The Department desires that energetic and thorough
inquiry be made of the circumstances surrounding the
"series of dinners, entertainments and barbecues" report-
ed to have been given by Japanese Consuls, with a view to
ascertaining whether any statements of the substance and
character described in the report under reference have
been made by Japemese Consuls in Mexico, and, if so, to
obtaining first-hand accounts of suoh statements.
It is desired in commection with this matter that
you avall yourself of the assistanoe of those Consuls, who,
in ter

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
RCOEIVEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF
August 1, 1934.
Mexico City's despatch No. 1582 of
July 7, 1934, reports the receipt of an
instruction directing that an investigation
be made of reports alleging the spread of
anti-American propaganda by Japanese consuls
in certain parts of Mexico, and procedes to
give a rather interesting account of
Japanese activities in Mexico.
I suggest that you read this despatch
for background purposes against the receipt
of a report which may be expected in due
course on the activities of Japanese
consular officers in that country.
The despatch is not susceptible of
intelligent comment from the point of view
of this Division; but in general one is
disposed to concur in the conclusions of
the Embassy at Mexico City, as set forth
in the last two paragraphs of the despatch,
that the probabilities are that Mexico
would side with us rather than with Japan
in the event of "grave consequences".

Department of State
10/11/34
The Honorable
fleting
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
I have the honor to acknowledge the Department's
strictly confidential instruction No. 403 of June 28,
1934, on the above subject, and to state that I have
initiated an inquiry regarding the spreading of anti-
American propaganda by Japanese Consuls stationed along
the West Coast of Mexico, the results of which I shall
report as early as possible.
In this regard, I have the honor to make certain ob-
servations regarding Japanese activities in Mexico. At
F/ESP
the present time Japanese agents and nationals are
busily employed in studying the topography and the com-
mercial resources of the country, in spreading Japanese
FILED
16 1934
propaganda, and in peaceful penetration.
This activity centers in the Japanese Legation at
Mexico

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Mexico City, with ramifications throughout the country.
Numerous Japanese, including the Military and the Naval
Attachés, travel a great deal in Mexico, maintaining
contacts beyond the capital.
The Department already is aware of Japanese recon-
naissance operations on the West Coast of Mexico. Recent-
ly, the Embassy has had two reports of the discovery of
well-organized Japanese surveying parties at work on the
West Coast - one somewhere between azatlán and Acapulco;
the second in the southern part of the State of Sinaloa,
north of Mazatlán. Both are reported to have disappeared
after their whereabouts was discovered.
INo reports have
reached the Embassy of any attempt by the exican Govern-
ment to apprehend the members of these parties, or to
curtail their activities. The Japanese Naval Attaché,
not long ago, was reported to have been discovered taking
photographs of the port of Manzanillo, when the latter
was being considered as a Mexican naval base.
The
Japanese inister was said to have offered to cause the
recall of the attaché, but the matter was dropped.
Undoubtedly, there is great Japanese activity in pros-
pecting for copper, metallic mercury, scrap iron, nitrates
and oil, with the apparent objective of acquiring consi-
derable quantities of these materials.
as regards oil,
a prime Japanese need, the £mbassy has it on good author-
ity that Japanese interests, believed to be supported by
the Japanese Government, are negotiating through a Japanese
business man in Lexico City for an oil concession in the
South-west of the State of Guerrero, the land involved
stretching to the coast, where there is a fair harbor.
The same Japanese agent also is reported to have attempt-
ed

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
ed to gain control of the production of a metallic-mer-
cury mine in Central Mexico.
As far as it has come to the Embassy's attention,
Japanese propaganda has followed commercial and self-
complacent lines. It is more or less obtrusive, remi-
niscent of pre-war German progapanda, and tends to in-
fluence favorably both the upper classes and the masses.
The former are given impressions of Japan's political
might, strength of race, industrial organization and
culture; the latter are appealed to by the argument
that Japan's primary interest is in supplying cheap,
useful goods to the masses while other competitor coun-
tries cater only to the favored classes by marketing ex-
pensive articles, primarily luxuries. This propaganda
is carried on by illustrated brochures, paid newspaper
articles, and propagandists (such as Dr. Kinta Arai
794120212 /3 461 3.19. 351 352
see despatch dated January 1i, 1934, from the Consulate
at Guaymas, and subsequent despatches from border Consul-
ates), the substance of which is echoed by Japanese resi-
dents in general.
The personal feature has an excellent
vehicle in the personality of the Japanese nationals who,
in general, from the  inister down, appear to have a
facility for establishing friendly relations with exicans.
I am not aware of the diffusion of direct anti-
American propaganda, but a great deal is spread by innu-
endo.
The thesis of this is that the possibility of war
between the United States and Japan does exist, that
Japan will be the victor in such a conflict and that a
Mexico friendly to Japan will reap therefrom a great re-
ward.
To date, however, and on excellent authority, I
have it that Japan expects Mexico to s tand with, or at
least

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
least to be sympathetic to, the United States in the
event of hostilities between the latter and Japan. It
is this fact which "the Prussian
of the East" now is
attempting to circumvent. In his activities towards this
end, he must be watched assiduously and checkmated.
工
do not believe that the Mexican Government will a ssume
an attitude inimical to the United States in the latter's
relations with Japan as long as General Calles and the
"Sonora Gang" are in control, but the day may dawn, in
a not distant future, when politicians less sympathetic
to the United States hold the reins of power, in conse-
quence of which a divergence of attitude is conceivable.
The peaceful penetration of Mexico by Japanese goes
on apace by their increasing legal and illegal entry.
During the years 1931, 1932, and the first half of 1933,
809 Japanese were legally admitted into exico; 436 derart-
ures were officially recorded; the net gain was 373 souls.
Of this number, 229 settled in the Northern District of
Baja California.
Frequent reports have come to me that
numerous Japanese constantly are entering the country
illegally, the majority going to Baja California and the
West Coast of the mainland. A near scandal in the Minis-
try of Gobernación was rumored because it was reported
that since the beginning of the current year, 6o0 Japanese,
said to be in the Japanese military service, had entered
Mexico from Guatemala bearing entry c ards, the report of
which and the proceeds of which had never reached the
superior Mexican authorities.
While an exact count of the number of Japanese now
residing in Mexico has not yet been made, I have it on
good authority that the official estimate of the number
is

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.95)
-5-
is 8,609 (not including those naturalized), of which
1,200 are resid ing in the Northern Dis trict of Baja
California.
Although the above circumstances in their aspect
to the United States cannot now be considered alarming,
they bear constant and c areful scrutiny and, in this
ttel
regard, I subscribe to the spirit of the Embassy's
711.1211/265c8
despatch No. 2205 of February 7, 1933
I do not be-
0 W ar
lieve that, irrespective of transit ory circumstances,
Mexico will adopt other than an attitude of friendship
vis-a-vis the United States. Trade and cultural rela-
1my
tions and propinquity alone tend to bind the two co un-
tries by ties oertainly closer than those which might
be drawn between Mexico and a remote country like Japan,
with which the former's commercial relations alone are
not comparable - even considering the existence of a
treaty between them which contains a most-favored-
nation clause - to those existing between her and the
United States.
Despite a certain amount of recurrent rumor that
a state of close rapprochement exists between Mexico and
Japan, I discount it in large measure and subscribe to
the expression of a prominent Mexican who is close to the
Mexican Government and to the Japanese Legation, and who
characterized such rapprochement as "more apparent than
real".
Respectfully yours,
R. Iy houd

AN 0 1935
Washington, D. C.
AMER  CAFTIGS
Dear Sir:
My calling of travelling salemman in the states of the Pacifie coast,
in this country, has enabled me to observe at close hand the aetivities of
the large Japanese colony established in these states and from the observa-
89
tions I have arrived at the conclusion that nearly all the Japanese settled
in this part of Mexico are using their commereial activities, etc., as a
4
mere cover for their actual purpose, which, in my opinion (and alse the
opiniom of many other persons), is none other than militery espionage di-
2
reeted against the United States and Mexioo. In this way they shcw themselves
0
ungrateful for the hospitality they are offered.
2
I am a Merican and a lover of my country and I understand that the
2
destiny of my country is bound up with that of the United States, both because
of the nearness of the two countries to each other and because of the common 
danger that threatens them in case Japan should be victorious (a remote possi-
bility) in a war against you. In this unfortunate case my country, without a 4
doubt, would be colonized by Japanese who would then proseed to practiee their
despotio and barbarous acts, so well shown in the ease of China. For thome
reasons I feel that as a Mexican I should be neglesting my duty if I did not
call your attention to the activity of the Japanese on the west coast of Mexieo.
In case of an armed confliet (a remote possibility but not improbable) these
activities would give rise to serious and dangerous conflicts both for you and
for us.
Two years ago when the Manchuria trouble reached its culminating point
F
and everybody feared a general conflict, many Japanese left for Japan in
answer to the call of their goverment. These were, without a doubt, soldiers
of the active army. I have in mind the case of a bakery laborer, apparently
G
an obseure and insignificant man, who turned out be nothing less than a mili-
tary engineer and who had come to Mexico to engage in this humble occupation,
so out of harmony with his calling and edueation. Without a doubt he had a
special commission from his goverment and it is logical to suppose that he
came for no other purpose than to act as a spy, and we find many cases similar
to this ome. There are many Japanese business houses that are failures finan-
cially and that do business at a loss and in spite or the fact that they are
mall firms it is impossible to explain how they can contimue to do busines
for so long a time unless they are finaneed by a third party, which it is to be
supposed would be their own government.
1935
-1-

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.97)
Last year a Japamese lesturer made a trip throughout the states of
the Pasifie eeast, siving free leetures in all the towns and showins seme
mavée fikes, bat the main theme of his lestares was that Japen was the
greet friend ef Mexiee and that its fleet and army were ready to defend it
in ease of danger, a statement whieh, mfortunately, is taksn sericusly by
a lasge part or the pepulation. of course the people who take this atti-
tade are me qbservers of events and kmow nothing of histery. When he
shewed the Tilms illustrating the maneuvers of the Japanese army and navy
he always stated that they were at the servise of Mexico and in ease of
meed fer wpiversal justiee. On these oesasions he was applauded by about
half of the audiesse.
We frequemtly have fishing expeditions frem Japan, with erews exelusively
Japamese. These expeditions are ostensibly for fishing purposes but what
they really do is make drawings of our coast and make soundings, semething
whieh the Govermment of Mexiso, wafortumately, is unable to prevent due to
the fact that it does not have the material means (ships, ete.) to wateh its
sheres as it should.
What I wish to tell yeu in this respeot is that I have been told by a
perses whem I suppose to be well informed that on an unsettled part of the
shere a Japamese boat seeretly unloaded diamounted aviation material and
they have, without a doubt, hidden this material in some of the uninhabited
seetiems, so frequently met with along our coast, with a view to using it in
ease of a future eemfliet. In case of a war they could mount this material
and mam it with forees which could do great damage to some of the eities of
both countries, even though the aviators, in attempting this, would only be
committing suicide.
I wish to suggest that you take measures to counteract this activity and
at the same time undertake, taetfully and intelligently, to show the Mexiean
people the delusion nder which they are laboring. By this I meam the erreneous
belief that Japan can help them. In addition to this you should explain to
the Maxican people the rapaoity of Japan and ber barbarous acts and make them
see that their destiny, for tarious reasons, is closely bound up with yours
and that a disaster for the one would mean a disaster for both.
r I can give you any information that would be of use along this line,
you may write, using an envelope without heading and addressing your letter
to apartado lio, Mazatlan, Sin., Mexico, and recamending the due reserve.
Yours truly,
Elias Garcia Cuadra
P. S. Please consider this letter as strictly confidential.

and taat the Mexican Government, wishing to give the

With particular refurence to my despatch No. 48 of
Sep tember 10, 1934, and my telegran of the same date, the
consulate has definite information that the SS BANSHUMARU
#88 left Shimonoseki, Japan, on January 3, 1935, bound for
Guaymas, Sonora, Mexico, via Yokohame.
The vessel carried
a crew of 43 men, includirg the master.
Apparently half
the men are listed as fishermen and the other half as
seamen and officers of the ship.
The BANSHUMARU belongs
to the firm of Hayashikane Shoten, of Shimonoseki, Japan,
which is the same conc ern as t at mentioned in my comruni-
cations of September 10, 1934.
The local American manager
of Pan-Aporican Fisheries. Ltd., tells ne that he has no
Instructions to deliver s rimp to the Japarese boat and that
his company expects to continue shipping shrimp to the
Uni ted States.
According to information furnished by him,
the BANSHUMARU is a vessel oi about 1,ooo tons, equipped for
transporting around 500 tons of fish.
My informant expressed
the opinion that the vessel went to Yokohama from Shimonseki
but did not leave Japan for Mexico, since the American and
Japanese companies could not agree on the final conditions
of the sale of the shrimp.
A local official furnished me information relative to the
vessel's expected arrival and mentioned that in view of the
presence in Guaymas recently of a Japanese traveling salesman
it would be interesting to note whether the vessel brought
any cargo from Japan to Mexioo.
He remarked that the
Japanese might possibly find it profitable to import merchandise
from Japan and take shrimp on the return voyage.
It is understood
that

transportation of merchandise, but inasmuch as the Japanese

Another theory advanced in Guaymas,
where there is apparently considerable discussion among
the Mexican residents regarding Japanese activities, is that
the BANSHUMARU is now overdue at this port and hat it may
have stopped at Magdalena Bay to unload iurther material
for fortifications there.
As previously reported, three old Mexican vessels,
the BOLIVAR, WASTINGTON, and MOSTEZULa, are being dis-
mantled in the port of Guaymas for the stated purpose of
shipping the metal to Japan.
The local press reports
that the represe tative of the Japanese concern purchasing
the metal has requested permission from the Mexican Govern-
ment to bring twelve old ships bought in tie United States
to Guymas to be dismentled here.
The metal obtained
would then be shipped to Japan along with that taken from
the Mexican vessels, and several hundred tons of scrap iron
said to have been purchased from the Southern Pacific Railwey
of Mexico.
On January 26, 1935, Mr. Tada iro Tamak1, who stated
that he was a Japanese subject living in the United States
and employed by the Fairbanks-Morse Company as a salesman
for engines and machinery, applied at this consulate for
assistance in obtaining permission to remain outside the
United States for an additional period of six months. He
had left the Uni ted States via Nogales on July 30, 1934.
He was informed that the consulate had no authority to extend
the period during which he was allowed to remain in Mexico
and

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
return to the United States without a visa.
Mr. Tamaki
tated that he was going to Nogales, enter the United States,
and thon return to Mexico for another period of approximetely
six months.
According to information furnished this consulate, Mr.
Tamaki goes to Baja California every year for about six months
duri the winter to engege in the growing of tomatoes. He
is quoted as having stated that the Japanese Government
wanted all Japanese in Baja California and southern Sonora
to engage in the growing of cotton instead of tomatoes as
Japan needed the cotton.
Tamaki is said to have made a
trip to Ciudad Obregón, the center of the Yaqui River Valley
agricultural region vhere a number of Japanese farmers live.
He is quoted as having said that he had advised the Japanese
in the Yaqui Valley to plant cotton instead of winter vege-
tables.
He returned to Baja California via Guaymas on
February 6, 1935.
The interest of the Japanese in cotton
might be explained by a desire to provide cargo for vessels
bringing merchandise from Japan to Mexico.
The importation
of Japanese goods into Mexico would naturally be more profitable
if the vessls transporting the merchandise could be furni hed
with cargo for the return trip to Japan.
A local official who furnished me part of the information
given above expressed the view that the Japanese were making
a determined effort to estab ish themselves on the west coast
of Mexioo, that they were particularly interested in the Gulf
of California, and that if they came here for the ostensible
purpose of fishing they were certain to engage in activities
which would be subversive to the interests of both Mexioo
and the Uni ted States.
This consulate views with concern the recent inorease of
activities

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
ities on the part of the Japanese in this istrict,
realizes that it may be difficult for the Government
of the Uni ted States to take any definite stand on the
matter. Nevertheless, it secns that it might be easier to
take certain steps now than later, such as informel discussions
with the Mexican Government Wi th a view to inducing the latter
to adopt a policy of excluding the Japanese as much as
possible from the west coast of Mexico and refusing to grant
fishing and other concessions.
Such a course might possibly
avoid serious trouble in the future for both the Uni ted
States and Mexico.
Respectfully yours,

I have the honor to refer to my strictly confidential
despatoh No. 87, of February 11, 1935, relative to Japanese
Aotivities in the Gulf of California and on the West Coast
of Mexioo, and to uote below a letter received from an
American citizen living on the west coast of Baja Cali-
fornia in reply to an inquiry made by this consulate
regarding the report that a foreign power had landed
cement, cannon, and ammunition in Magdalena Bay:
Your letter of January 2lst received.
The
Mexican Government is starting to build at
Magdalena Bay a naval base for warships, whioh
they are building in Spain. At the present
time, they are moving cement and other materials
F/FG
to Magdalena.
This information I have known
for some time and have oheoked it with General
Juan Dominguez, the Governor, not letting him
know you were making these inquiries.
Any further information, I will be glad
to get for you.
The

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
The above quoted letter, dated March 3rd, is from
a native-born American citizen who has been known to
this consulate for some time and who is believed to be
a reliable source of information.
In my despatch under reference above, I remarked
on page 2: "It seems surprising that the Japanese, if
it is true that they landed such material in Magdalena
Bay, would choose a spot where a Mexican naval base is
now being established in preparation for ships to be
obtained for the Mexican Navy."
In view of that
and the letter quoted above, it seems practically
certain that no foreign power has recently landed
cement, cannon, and ammunition at Megdalena Bay, and
that the materials which caused the reports were in
reality put there by the Mexican Government. However,
for a time the rumors were so consistent the consulate
felt they should be reported.
Respectfully yours,
JmmRan
Guy W. Ray,
American Vace Consul.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 26, 1935.
The Consul at Guaymas transmits an
article appearing in a local paper relative
to Japanese activities along the west coast
of Mexico. Japan is pictured as a menace
to the peace of Mexico which, it is pointed
out, would become a second Belgium in case
of war between the United States and Japan.
The Consul declares that the unusual
feature of this article lies not so much
in the information contained therein as
in the fact that it should be published at
all in that district.
A Mexican naval officer informed the
Consul that, in connection with the rumor
that the Japanese had landed munitions in
Magdalena Bay, he had made a thorough
investigation and was satisfied that no
basis in fact for the rumor existed.
The Mexican Government has officially
informed the Pan-American Fisheries, Ltd.,
an American company, that it has no objec-
tion to the visit of a Japanese ship to
Guaymas for loading shrimps for shipment
to Japan.

I have the honor to transmit herewith copies of
MAy95
th
 Spanish text and an informal translation of an
ticle which appeared in the HERALDO DEL YAQUI,
2
Ciudad Obregón, Sonora, Mexico, of April 17, 1935,
relative to Japanese activities along the west coast
of Mexico. The unusual feature of the article lies
FILED
not so much in the nature of the information contained
5
as in the fact that it should be published at all in
35
this district. Most of the statements made in the
article are nothing more than what most observers in
this part of the world have confidently believed for
a long time, although definite proof as to the
absolute truth of some of the allegations might be
difficult to seoure.
During recent years, it has been notable here
F/FG.
that the press was quick to criticize Americans and
the

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the Government of the United States, but has been
extremely careful regarding what it had to say about
the Japanese. It is worthy of note that in this
article a definite stand is taken against the Japanese
where it is intimated that the Mexican Government does
not approve of the policy now being followed by Japan,
but that it is on muoh better terms with the United
States than has been the case with recent administra-
tions.
Considerable criticism has been heard in Guaymas
recently of the commercial methods employed by Japan
and fear has been expressed that the Japanese would
make a conoerted effort to invade the Mexican market.
However, so far the looal press has made no mention of
the subject.
With regard to the Japanese subject mentioned in
612.9417
my despatoh No. 83, of February 4, 1935, as having
visited Guaymas and other cities in southern Sonora
selling miscellaneous novelties, a local official told
me recently that he had received two visits from the
Japanese in question who had impressed him as not being
an ordinary salesman. My informant said he remarked
particularly that his visitor walked into the room
erect, clicked his heels together, and saluted briskly
in a military fashion. He added that the Japanese in
question must have been a naval or army officer as his
bearing was in marked oontrast to the ordinary Japanese
civilian who bows, sorapes, and sucks air through his
teeth.
▲

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
3
A Mexican naval officer informed me voluntarily
in a casual onversation a few days ago, that he had
investigated the report ciroulated in the early part
of this year that the Japanese had landed cement, arms,
and ammunition in the Bay of Magdalena, on the west
coast of Baja California, presumably for the purpose of
erecting fortifications there. In this conneotion
please see my Strictly Confidential despatoh No. 87, of
February 1l, 1935. My informant told me that, with
other Mexican officials he had made a thorough
investigation in Magdalena Bay and the surrounding
territory and that they were satisfied there was no
foundation whatsoever for the report that a foreign
power had landed material there. He went on to say
that, personally and quite unofficially, he was
suspicious of every action of the Japanese in this part
of the world, and expressed the opinion that their
activities were seldom, if ever, what they appeared to
be on the surface.
So far as the consulate is able to learn, no
Japanese has visited Guaymas since the gentleman
894.20212365
mentioned in my despatoh No. 87, of/February 11, 1935.
The manager of Pan-American Fisheries, Ltd., an
American company operating in Guaymas, informs me that
the Mexican Government has officially informed his
firm that it has no objection to having a Japanese
vessel come to Guaymas and load shrimp for shipment to
Japan, and that probably a Japanese ship will come to
Guaymas

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
4
Guaymas next fall to take a load of some 50o tons of
shrimp to Japan.
With the present wave of nationalistio feeling,
there is a general resentment against foreign labor and
capital in this district. However, the attitude towards
Americans in this district is probably more friendly
than it has ever been. If it were a case of ohoosing
between the United States and Japan, there seems to be
little doubt that general sentiment would favor the
former. The strong desire of the inhabitants here to
attraot American tourists has some influence on their
attitude, and there also seems to be a general feeling
that the present administration in the United States
has a sincere desire to show its friendship for Mexioo.
As has been reported previously, there are no
Japanese in Guaymas. However, there are a number of
Japanese farmers in the Yaqui Valley, probably about 7
or 8 and none of them oultivate large acreages. There
are also a few Japanese merchants in Ciudad Obregón.
There appears to be a larger colony in Navojoa, and it
is reliably reported that the Japanese Consul at
Mazatlán makes frequent trips to Navojoa, and that
each time he goes there the Japanese residents hold a
meeting at which he is of course present. The colony
in Navojoa is said to be organized and to hold a
meeting at least twice a month. Navojoa is some
distance from the sea and does not appear to be a
particularly strategio point from a military angle.
The

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
The significance of activities of the Japanese in
Navojoa is probably not as sinister as some of the
inhabitants there would like to believe.
However, from reports reaching this consulate
from time to time, it seems certain that the well
known interest of the Japanese in the Gulf of
Califomia and the west oast of Mexico is not inspired
solely by the possibility of commercial profit.
The HERALDO DEL YAqUI, published in Ciudad Obregón,
has a very limited circulation in Guaymas. However,
two residents of this city telephoned me yesterday
hinting that that paper carried a prominent article on
the front page which might be of interest to me. They
remarked that the publication of the article was a
surprise to them, and that the fact that a Merican
paper had brought the matter out into the open might
be significant.
Respectfully yours,
Guy W. Ray,
American Vice Consul.

BAJA CALIFORNIA, AREA DE COMBÁTE
Japon Planea una Preparación Para Conseguir Tenerla
Al Menos Tal se Viene Asegurando en las Estados Unidos
del Norte a Causa de las Miras de Hegemonía en el
Pacífico que se Disputan
¿Será México en Dicho Caso Otra Bélgica Obligada a

(Solo para este Periódico)
NOGALES, abril 16. Uno de las problemas más sinceros
del Japón es atacar a los Estados Unidos, a través de una
táctica. que pueda deslizarse por los más eminentes puntos
estratégicos de la Baja California; este es el punto de
observación de las propagandas oficiosas de los
centinelas de esta nación en disturbio que se llama Norte-
america. Este punto de..vista fue tambión sustentado por
el inquieto repórter, repleto de espíritu de aventura,
Floyd Gibbons, cuando hace algunos años publicó un libro
de pesadillas magnírico que tituló El Napoleón.Rojo. En
su libro de destrucción de Estados Unidos por los
amarillos se inicia de una manera tempestuosa e inconce-
bible, por los cauces sin dique en la Baja California.
BAJA CALIFORNIA EN PELIGRO
Según hay oomprobaciones indubitables, el Japón
mantiene.en las costas oeste de México un perfecto.
"sistema de inteligencia", compuesto orgánicamente por
dueños de tiendas de todas clases, restaurantes, agri-
cultores y pescadores.
Según las recientes declaraciones del periodista
norteamericano Fred Pesley, que viene publicando una
serie de artíoules con respecto a las actividades del
Japón en Arizona, las Costas del Pacífico y las Islas
del.Hawai, el Japón tenía debidamente estudiado el
establecer un campo de aterrizaje en Ensenada, Baja
California, estableciendo allí una base aérea, a
cincuenta minutos de San Diego y dos horas de Los
Angeles.
El Embajador norteamericano en México, Josephus
Daniels, tal parece que poseído de la.misma preocupación
envió un agente a la Ba ja Califormia, con el objeto de.
indagar entre las familias norteamericanas, sobre
cualquier Indole de asunto que pudiera tener relación
con ello. .Esas son algunas de las cosas que ocurren,
bajo la aparente quietud urbana del pintoresco pueblo
de Caléxico, aledaño a la frontera y bajo la égida del
Valle Imperial, exactamente al cruzar una calle de
Mericali,

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.98)

Mexicali, que es la capital norteña del Distrito de la
Baja California.
SE HABLA DE LA
GUERRA
Mericali, famoso particularmente entre los norte-
americanos que afluyen a sus dancings, sus casas de
juego, sus cantinas, a beber el recio y substantivo
tequiia y a reflejar sus rostros ingenuos y burdamente
felices en el fondo negro y paradojal de la juventud

ouando llega el placer abre los brazos."
Pero también existe en Mexicali otro centro de
acción, menos festivo y olvidadizo, menos irresponsable
y charlatán. Ese es el foco de la política inter-
nacional y los negocios; allí existe también la
despepitadora de algodón más..grande del mundo, porque
allí los americanos tienen..cerca de un millón de acres
de algodón, en el delta del Río Colorado.
Allí en ese centro es donde los norteamericanos
aprenden.sobre política de México, en torno del Palacio
de Gobierno y en la plaza donde emerge con prominencia
herbica la estatua de Obregón; allí también aprenden

habla de la guerra del Japón con una seguridad que
pasma, con una displicencia fatalista. Rso lo saben
todos los mexicanos, japoneses y norteamericanos, los
que residen allí y los que visitan con el objeto de
saberlo.
EL ESPIONAJE JAPONES
Hay cerca de mil japoneses pescadores que trabajan
por toda la costa oeste en torno de Rnsenada. Se
afirma que cada uno de ellos es un espía inteligente,
hábil, temerario, patriota hasta el suicidio, que
anteponen el deber hasta la muerte. Además, se
asegura que en esas oostas la pesca es mezquina, de la
que es imposible lucrar, pero que los japoneses mantienen
la pesca, no obstante esa realidad, económicamente
negativa, pero táctica y estratégicamente ponderante.
Recuérdese cuando no hace mucho..tiempo veinte barberos
japoneses, inesperadamente, al pasar un barco japonés
por la Zona del Canal de Panama, aparecieron impecable-
mente vestidos de oficiales de.marina, mostrando una dis-
ciplina atildada y perfeota.
▲ ciertas millas, muy pocas por cierto, de la
ciudad de Mexicali, hay cerca de cincuenta mil acres
de terreno que resultan adecuadas para hacer la base
aérea a que nos referimos al principio y en las que se
piensa que los japoneses pretenden situar su ouartel
general. Barcos de guerra y toda olase de transportes
abreos,

VICTIIA COMO BELGICA
Cierto periodista decfa hace algunos días que el
Japón tendría tanto esorúpulo de invadir México como
Alemania de..invadir a Bélgica; asímismo afirmaba que
México estaría más indefenso para.repeler esa agre-
sión, que Bélgica a Alemania. Para ello argumenta que
Méxioo no tiene fortalezas y que no tiene un ejéroito
que pueda avalorar el nombre.
Abriendo trayectorias a grandes elucubraciones, 
he dicho además que el gobierno aotual de Mexico, no
admitiría bajo ningún motivo el que los japoneses
llegaran a orear situaciones muy delicadas en ese
sentido, porque además de no estar de acuerdo con la
politica japonese, la idea que tiene con respecto a
Estados Unidos es completamente distinta a la de otras
administraciones pasadas. También se ha puesto la
idea de que un Ejeroito siempre está en muy grave
peligro cuando pretende librar batallas de la magnitud
que serían esas, legos de su suelo.
Esos argumentos son sensatos para un sentido co-
mín que no tiene más misión que recoger las impresiones
superficiales de los pensamientos que no pueden penetrar
y analizar de acuerdo con las realidades que la ciencia
ha puesto a la disposición de la guerra. Porque cuando
un ejército lejos de su patria, va a pelear en las
circunstancias que acontecería con el Japón, visuali-
zando los peligros que se cernirán sobre el ejército
imperial, no sería cuerdo el pensar que viniera a un
suicidio deliberado.

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
TRANSLATION OF ARTICLE TAKEN FROM THE CIUDAD OBREGÓN
NEWBPAPER (HERAIBO DEL YAQUI) DATED
PRIL 17, 1935.
BAJA CALIFORNIA, ARRA OF COMBAT.
Japan Making Preparations to Hold it.
At least, So It is Stated in the United States to the
North Because of the Points of Hegemony which
are Disputed in the Pacific.
Will Mexico in That Case Be Another Belgium, Obliged to
Go To War solely in order Not to See Her
Territory Trampled by the Powers at War?
(Special for this Newspaper)
NOGALrS. April 16. One of the most genuine
problems of Japan is to attack the United States by
means of tactics which can be effected through the
most prominent strategic points of Baja California; such
is the point of view of the official propaganda of the
guardians of the disturbed nation to the north known as
North America. This point of view was also upheld by
the troubled reporter, Floyd Gibbons, filled with the
spdrit of adventure, when a few years back he published
a magnificent book of nightmares entitled "The Red
Napoleon." In his book of destruction of the United
States by the Yellow race, it begins in a tempestuous
and inconveivable manner, through the dikeless river
beds of Baja California.
BAJA CALIFORNIA IN DANGER.
According to incontrovertible proofs, Japan
maintains on the west coast of Mexico a perfect "system
of intelligence", comprised of omers of shops of all
kinds, restaurants, farmers, and fishermen.
According to the recent deolarations of the North
American newspaperman, Fred Pesley, who is publishing
a series of articles regarding the activities of Japan
in Arizona, the Pacifio Coasts, and the Island of Hawaii,
Japan has carefully studied the establishment of a landing
field in Insenada, Baja California, creating a base there
for airplanes, fifty minutes from San Diego and two hours
from Los Angeles.
The North American Ambassador to Mexico, Josephus
Daniels, apparently having the same worries sent an
agent to Baja California with the object of investiga-
ting among the North American families regarding anything
that might have a bearing on the subjeot. Those are
some of the things that happen, beneath the apparent
urban tranquillity of the pioturesque city of Calexioo,
near

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
near the frontier under the aegis of the Imperial
Valley, exactly across the street from Mexicali, which
is the northern capital of the District of Baja
California.
TALK OF WAR
Mexicali is famous, particularly among the North
Americans who flock to its danoe halls, its gambling
houses, to drink the strong and substantial tequila and
to reflect their naive and happy eyes in the dark
depths of feminine youth which "when pain comes lowers
its face, and when pleasure arrives opens its arms."
But there also exists in Mexicali another center
of action, less festive and forgetful, less irresponsible
and idle. That is the center of international politios
and business; there exists also the biggest cotton gin
in the world, because it is there that the Americans
have about a million acres of cotton, in the delta of
the Colorado River.
There, in that center, is where the North Americans
learn about Mexican politios, around the Government
Palace and in the square where stands so prominently and
heroically the statue of Obregón; there also the North
Americans, agents of the Government and ambitious
newspaper reporters, learn about the activities of Japan.
There war with Japan is discussed with a certainty which
astonishes, with a fatalistic displeasure. That is
known by all Mexicans, Japanese, and North Americans,
those who live there and those who visit with the objeot
of learning it.
JAPANESE ESPIONAGE.
There are about 1,0o0 Japanese fishermen who work
along all the west coast round about Ensenada. It is
stated that everyone of them is a spy, intelligent,
clever, fearless, patriotio even to the point of
suicide, that they place duty ahead of death. Further-
more, it is stated that along these coasts fishing is
poor, that profit is impossible, but the Japanese
maintain the fishing, in spite of that reality,
economically negative, but tactically and strategically
powerful. Remember not long ago when twenty Japanese
barbers, unexpectedly, on the occasion of the passage of
a Japanese ship through the Panama Canal Zone, appeared
impeccably dressed as naval officials, showing a polished
and perfect discipline.
A few miles, certainly very few, from the city of
Mexicali, there are about 50,000 acres of land whioh .
would be adequate for the air base to which we
referred

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
referred at the beginning and on whioh the Japanese
propose to locate their general headquarters. Warships
and all kinds of air transports can be concentrated
normally about twelve miles from Ensenada in the Bay
of Todos Santos. All the rest would be absolutely
simple.
VICTIM LIKE BERGIUM
A certain newspaperman said a few days ago that
Japan would have as much soruple in invading Mexico as
Germany had in invading Belgium; also he afrirmed that
Mexioo would be more defenseless than Belgium was
against Germany. For that, he argued that Mexic∞ has
no forts and that it has no army worthy of the name.
Giving ground for serious thought, it has been
said also that the present government of Mexico would
not allow under any pretext that the Japanese succeed
in creating delicate situations of that kind, because
besides not being in accord wi th the Japanese polioy, the
idea that it has with regard to the United States is
completely different from that of other past administra-
tions. Also the idea has been advaneed that an army
is always in very grave danger when it endeavors to
engage in battles of the magnitude such as those would
be, far from its territory.
These arguments are reasonable for a common sense
that has no mission other than to gather the superficial
impressions of thoughts which cannot penetrate and
analyze in accord with the realities which science has
placed at the disposal of war. Because when an army,
far from its country, goes to fight in the circumstanoes
which would be the case with Japan, visualizing the
dangers which would surround the Imperial Army, it
would not be logioal to think that it was coming to
a deliberate suicide.

it might have on the defense of the Panama Canal.

matters for the consideration of the Mexican Division,

indicated that he would do so, and on leaving my office
went, I believe, to that Division.

SUBJECT: Japanese Activities in Mexico
TO:
The Assistant Chief of Staff, G-2, Wer Departmont,
Weshington, D.C.
1. The following confidentiel report was made to me by Colonel
894.202
Walter B. iyron, Vice  resident of the Gulr Refining Company in Moxico.
"ror severel years the Jepanese Government or repre-
sentatives of that governnent heve been rather active in
search for oil in Mexico. Their activities have largely
894.20212
been in what we term the Isthmus Country south and south-
east of Tampico. They have acquired a considerabl• num-
ber of oil concessions in that territory, and apparently
2
in individuals' names have acquired some concessions in
the states of Temaulipas and Nuevo Leon. hocently, their
C2
movements indicate that they may start some developmont
88
work in the Isthmus Country. At present there is : rail-
0
road from the Fort of Mexico on the Bay of Campeche to
Saline Cruz on the Gulf of Tehuantepec. The development
will be somewhat along this line, probably in the southorn
part of Veracruz. If oil were found at this point, it
could be trensported by tank cars to Salina Cruz. The
Mexican Government is now dredging e ship channol that
will acconnodate ocean-going vessels at alina Cruz.
"curfece indicetions for oil are very good in the
above-described aree and it is possible that oil in
commercial quantities mey be developed."
2. Colonel ryron also stated that labor demands upon his company
in Mexico are becoming increesingly difficult. He cited the two fol-
lowing examples - that the company discharge five of its key employee
in Mexico and that all Mexicen employees discharged since 1930 be re-
employed by the company.
(Sgd.)
F. B. MALLON.

u.
1710
Areview of the correspondence in the Department'e
ftres fo the last three years has disclosed no reports,
2
5
otier than one or two based on unconfirmed rumors, of
894.20212/38
an intensification of Japanese activities on the West
Coast of Mexico, apart from apparently legitimate fish-
ing enterprises. There are no reports indicating that
there has been an abnormally large influx of Japanese
into Baja California in recent years, and in view of
8
the fact that American consulates are maintained at
Ensenada and Mexicali, Baja California, and at Guaymas,
which is situated on the mainland just across the Gulf
from the lower portion of the peninsula, it is believed
that the absence of such reports may be considered as
reassuring on the point.
With respect to the statement that Japanese nationals
enjoy a privileged status with respect to immigration
into Mexico, it is understood that for a time they were
in
F/F
G

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
in fact entitled by treaty to claim certain exemptions
from the exclusive provisions of the Mexican immigration
laws. However, the Department's information indicates
that the provision of the treaty between Mexico and
Japan which conferred such privileged status upon na-
tionals of the latter country was terminated by an
agreement entered into by the two Governments in the
year 1924.
Edward L. Reed.

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Division of the American Republios.
MEMORANDUM
August 4, 1937.
Mr. Duggan:
Recently rumors were current in
various quarters in Washington that there
were known to be about 100,000 Japanese
in Mexico. The War Department thereupon
asked the Department of State to make
a careful check through our Consuls
in Mexico. Accordingly, the Consul
General at Mexico City was instructed
to prepare a confidential report, en-
listing for that purpose the services
of all American Consuls in Mexico if
necessary. Consul General Stewart's
confidential report was received a few
days ago placing the estimated number
of Japanese in Mexico slightly above
5000. Do you wish to see the report?
87-

(a) A map showing the limitations of the various
consular districts in Mexico is attached.
(b) At Guadalajara, Mazatlán, Mexico City, Piedras
Negras, Tampico and Veracruz there are no figures avail-
able for the number of Japanese immigrating into those
districts during the past two years. In each case, how-
ever, the statement is made that the number is believed
to be negligible.
(c) These estimates (350 and 40) were supplied by
Japanese sources and, according to the Consul at Torreón,
are believed to be high by American and Mexican sources.

tion of Mexicali. Mexican immigration figures show 532

*See page 3, paragraph 2.

=== Page 321 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
According to reports received from the various
consulates, most of the Japanese are engaged as petty
meohnts, farmers and fishermen, and a few as barbers,
doctors and dentists.
The estimated number of Japanese in Mexico was
arrived at by taking the number (3,664) estimated by
the consuls (except those in Lower Calirornia) and
adding thereto the estimate (1,650) for Lower California
which was supplied by the Secretary of the Japanese
Legation to a Secretary in the American Embassy, making
a total of 5,314. Thus, the lower figure of 1,400
supplied by the consuls was not used.
Mexican Census Figures
The Mexican census for 1930 gives
the mumber of Japanese in Mexico as......4,310
Immigrants entering Mexico from Janu-
ary, 1931, to November 1, 1936
(Office of Social Statistics).........
689
TOTAL.
.4,999
2/
According to the attached statement from the
Office of Social Statistics, 180 Japanese left the coun-
try during the above-mentioned period. Being legal
residents, they had the right to return witnin two
years. However, there are no figures available as to
how many actually returned. Nor are there figures
available showing how many Japanese entered Mexico
illegally during that period.
Comparative Census figures for the years 1900,
1910, 1921 and 1930 are as fo11ows:

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)

According to the above, 1579 female Japanese
entered Mexico in 1930. This figure may be high as
in 1930 a Mexican woman who married a foreigner ac-
quired his nationality and it may be that the figure
includes some Mexican-born women.
While in numerous cases it was impossible for
the consular officers to obtain accurate figures
regarding the number of Japanese at present in their
districts, nevertheless, the data were acquired in-
dependently and, therefore, it is believed that the
figures may be considered as a fairly reliable check
on the Census figures and the estimates supplied by
Japanese sources.
With reference to the number of Japanese entering
Mexico during the last two years, the Consul at
Mazatlán reports that the procurement of immigration
visas is, and has been for several years, quite dif-
ficult. This statement is confirmed by several other
officers and their figures also tend to substantiate
the statement. In this connection, the immigration
authorities at Veracruz gave the following information
to the American Consul at that port:
"Japanese immigration is restricted at
present, and the only Japanese who enter the
Port of Veracruz are the spouses of residents
of Mexico or of Mexican citizens. Almost no
Japanese immigrants have landed at Veracruz
during the last five years. Orientals coming
to this section of the country enter at Mazat-
lán or at other Pacific ports."
From this study, it appears that only a small
number of Japanese has entered Mexico in recent
years

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.96)
-5
years and that the number living in Mexico at present
is about 5,300.
