=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.99)
El Soviet y el Japón
"Si el Japón se atreve a atacar la República de Mongolia
Exterior y pretende destruir su independencia, tenemos que
ayudar a esa República", dijo Stalin en la entrevista que con-
cedió el 1º de marzo de 1936, a Roy Howard, representante de
los períodicos Scripps-Howard, de los Estados Unidos. Apenas
pasaron dos semanas; el pacto soviet-mongol de mutua ayuda
se firmó en Ulambator el 12 del mismo mes, y su texto fué
publicado el 7 de abril próximo pasado. Estos hechos indican
la línea de política que la Rusia Soviética persigue con respec-
to a la Mongolia Exterior, política que no deja de afectar pro-
fundamente las relaciones soviet-japonesas, y que ha dado lu-
gar en todas partes a predicciones de inminente guerra entre
las dos naciones.
Durante los últimos años, después de la renovación de las
relaciones diplomáticas entre Japón y Rusia, en virtud de la
Convención de Pekín de 1925, las relaciones entre los dos paí-
ses han pasado por tres distintas etapas. La primera fué mar-
cada por favorables desarrollos, con el recíproco comercio
creciente año tras año, aunque en cada estación pesquera de
los mares del Norte se repitieron las disputas entre las dos
121

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
potencias. La segunda llegó en el otoño de 1990, a raíz del
incidente de Manchuria, y las relaciones soviet-japonesas to-
maron un repentino giro hacia lo peor, y muchas veces ame-
nasaban con una ruptura, debido & una serie de dificultades
en conexión con Manchukuo. A pesar de todo, la actitud de
Moseú se mostró conciliatoria, y la U. S. S. R. propuso un pac-
to de no-agresión al Japón, y ofreció la venta del Ferrocarril
Chino del Este, conjurando así una crisis.
La tercera etapa y el pésimo período en las relaciones so-
viet-japonesas, comenzó en los primeros meses del año pasado,
cuando estallaron las dificultades fronterizas entre Manchu-
kuo y Mongolia Exterior. No obstante la actitud conciliato-
ria hacia Manchukuo, Moscú está listo para recurrir a la gue-
rra contra todo designio sobre la Mongolia Exterior, como lo
evidencian las declaraciones de Stalin y el pacto soviet-mongol
de mutua ayuda antes mencionados. La paz del Extremo
Oriente parece ahora depender del problema de Mongolia Ex-
terior.
El problema de Mongolia, sin embargo, no es nuevo. La
Mongolia Exterior es geográficamente una mejor vecina de
Rusia que de China, y desde el punto de vista social-económico,
e una fácil presa a la sovietización. Esto está probado por va-
rios acontecimientos. En 1920, Urga fué invadida por una
banda de soldados blancos de Rusia, quienes a su vez fueron
desterrados de la capital mongólica por las tropas soviéticas,
y un nuevo régimen fué establecido por el Partido Popular de
Mongolia con la ayuda soviética. A este sacudimiento político
siguió la firma del tratado soviet-mongol, en Moscú, en el oto-
ño de 1921; tratado que, mientras reconoce la independencia
de la Mongolia Exterior, la pone bajo el gobierno virtual de
la Rusia Soviética.
122

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
Nominalmente, el gobierno de Mongolia Exterior queda en
manos del Partido Popular de Mongolia; pero el apoderamien-
to soviético de esta parte de China ha Hegado a ser notoria-
mente firme en los últimos quince años, especialmente desde
que el ala izquierda del referido Partido asumió el poder, algu-
nos años después de la inauguración del nuevo régimen. A
despecho de su obvio control de la Mongolia Exterior, Moscá
prefería, hasta hace poco, quedar detrás de la escena, y como
impedía al Gobierno de la Mongolia Exterior tener contacto
directo con cualquiera otra Potencia que no fuera Rusia So-
viética, las relaciones entre estos dos países han sido un com-
pleto misterio para los extraños. Esta política de secreto de
parte de Moscú fué probablemente dictada por los términos
del Pacto chino-soviet, negociado por el doctor Wellington
Eoo y M. Kharahan en Pekín en 1924, que asegura la sobera-
ría china sobre aquella región y, por esta razón, no animaba
s Moscú a obrar abiertamente respecto a la misma comarca
mongólica.
El abandono reciente de esta política de secreto por Moscú,
evidenciada por las declaraciones de Stalin y por la publica-
ción del Pacto de mutua ayuda, puede atribuirse en mucho 
tres circunstancias. En primer lugar, las relaciones entre Man-
chukuo y Mongolia han empeorado notoriamente, a cansa de
las dificultades fronterizas; en segundo, la situación del Ex-
tremo Oriente se ha desarrollado de tal manera, que marca
una clara división entre los grupos Rojo y anti Rojo de las na-
ciones; en tercero, la Rusia Soviética ha aumentado grande-
mente sus fuerzas de defensa, como resultado de sus sucesivos
planes quinquenales.
La frontera entre Manchukuo y Mongolia Exterior, que se
extiende a más de 700 kilómetros, no está claramente demar-
123

=== Page 459 === (confidence 1.00)
cada en varias secciones; pocos mongoles nómadas saben lo
que es una frontera; y existen pocos lindes naturales o artifi-
ciales. Por lo tanto, es natural que hayan sucedido perturba-
ciones en la frontera, entre el recién establecido imperio de
Manchukuo y la Mongolia Exterior. Las negociaciones diplo-
máticas han servido poco para arreglar semejantes disputas.
La Conferencia mongol-manchúa que se inició en Manchouli
en mayo de 1935, levantó sus sesiones después de seis meses,
sin ningún provecho. Los puntos principales de la agenda
eran el arreglo para el intercambio de plenipotenciarios di-
plomáticos entre los dos países, y el nombramiento de una
comisión permanente para resolver las disputas fronterizas er
el lugar. El fracaso final llegó cuando se supo que la Mon-
golia Exterior nunca estuvo dispuesta a recibir a un plenipo
tenciario del Manchukuo en Ulambator. Mientras tanto, d
Primer Ministro y el Ministro de Guerra de Mongolia Exte
rior visitaron Moscú hacia el fin del año pasado, dizque para
discutir las medidas de cooperación con los líderes soviéticos,
para resistir la presión del Japón y del Manchukuo. El mo-
vimiento autónomo que estalló casi al mismo tiempo en el Nor-
te de China, fué probablemente discutido por los líderes soviet-
mongoles durante sus conferencias.
Al regresar a Ulambator el Primer Ministro mongol y su
eomitiva, se agravó seriamente la situación, tanto en la fron-
tera manchú-mongólica como en el Norte de China. Estos cam-
bios aparentemente se combinaron para expeditar el pacto
soviet-mongol de mutua ayuda, firmado en Ulambator el 12 de
marzo de 1936. Casi simultáneamente, una columna del Ejér-
cito Rojo de China lanzó una expedición contra Shansi, segu-
ramente con el consentimiento de Nankín. Este nuevo des-
arrollo no dejó de atraer la atención de muchos observadores,
124

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
quienes ven en ello una indicación de que todo el Extremio
Oriente está siendo dividido en dos antagónicos bloques polí-
ticos, el uno rojo y el otro anti rojo, agregando de esta manera
otro peligro de contienda entre el Japón y la Unión Soviética.
El reciente aumento de recursos y armamento de Rusia,
que debe haber contribuído parcialmente a la tensión provo-
cada por el apoderamiento soviético de la Mongolia Exterior,
es realmente asombroso. El primer plan quinquenal, completa-
do al final de 1932, aumentó la producción industrial a 42 "bi-
llones" de rublos y la agrícola a 13.1 "billones", y estas cifras
van a subir a 52.7 y 62.2 "billones", respectivamente, para fi-
nes de 1937, al terminar el segundo plan quinquenal. Es muy
aparatoso el reciente progreso soviético en industrias pasadas;
de todos los países industriales del mundo, Rusia reclama
ahora el primer lugar en la manufactura de herramientas
agrícolas, el segundo en la producción de petróleo y hierro en
lingotes, el tercero en la fuerza eléctrica y acero, y el cuarto
en carbón. Tal progreso ha facilitado naturalmente el relleno
de armamentos, y el Gobierno móscovita desembolsó 5 "billo-
nes" de rublos en 1934, y 6.2 "billones" en 1935, para la de-
fensa nacional, y el presupuesto de 1936 para el mismo fin
sube a 14.8 billones de rublos. La Unión Soviética se jacta de
poseer el más grande ejército del mundo, al ser el Ejército
rojo permanente aumentado a dizque hasta 1.300,000 oficiales
y hombres en 1935, de los 940,000 que tenía en 1934.
Que la Rusia Soviética quiere estar preparada para pelear
contra el Japón y Alemania, ha sido hecho del dominio públi-
co por las autoridades soviéticas durante estos últimos años.
En el Séptimo Congreso Soviético y en la última sesión de la
Comisión Central Soviética, el Subcomisario de Guerra insistió
enfáticamente en la necesidad de prepararse contra los ataques
125

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
simultáneos por el Japón y Alemania. :Además, en todos sen-
tidos, con el propósito de defenderse contra estas dos poten-
eias, la Rusia Soviética se unió a la Liga de Naciones y adqui-
rió varios compromisos con otros países. Parece, pues, que
está alineándose con Francia contra Alemania, y con China
contra el Japón. En vez de ser una bete noire de la familia
de naciones, la Unión Soviética está ahora siendo amiga de
algunas potencias capitalistas, haciendo posible para ella el
hecho de verse envuelta en una guerra imperialista. Inciden-
talmente, su alineamiento con Francia y Checoeslovaquia en el
Oeste, y con Mongolia Exterior y el Ejército Rojo de China,
en el Este, puede posiblemente resultar en provecho de la Gran
Bretaña, los Estados Unidos y otros países. Sin codiciar ni
una pulgada de territorio extranjero, como asegura Stalin, la
Rusia Soviética puede que rechace todos los designios impe-
rialistas en su asociación con otras potencias; pero nadie sabe
qué resultados pueda traer su política actual. Ha llegado a
ser un factor en la línea imperialista, y nadie puede decir a
dónde la va a llevar esa actitud.
Así puede decir que las cinco diferentes líneas de desarro-
llo se combinan para apuntar a las adversas relaciones entre
el Japón y la Unión Soviética: en primer lugar, las relacio-
nes manchukuo-mongolas están volviéndose peores, debido a
las dificultades fronterizas; en segundo, las dificultades entre
Manchukuo y Mongolia conducirán a los mongoles a depender
más y más de Moscú para su cooperación; en tercero, el movi-
miento autónomo en el Norte de China y la expedición anti
Shansi del Ejército Rojo de China pueden posiblemente pre-
cipitar la división del Asia Oriental en dos grupos: Rojo y anti
Rojo, dirigidos respectivamente por la Rusia Soviética y el Ja-
pón; en cuarto, la Unión Soviética ha desarrollado últimamen-
126

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
te sus recursos, y su Ejército está preparado para cualquiera
emergencia. Finalmente Rusia ha perfeccionado su seguridad
en Europa con la firma de un pacto de mutua ayuda com
Francia, y está ahora en libertad para tomar una positiva ao-
ción en el Extremo Oriente.
Esto, sin embargo, no significa necesariamente un inmedia-
to peligro de guerra entre el Japón y la Rusia Soviética. Es
difícil concebir que la Unión Soviética emprenda una guerra
agresiva contra el Japón, por bien preparada que esté. Por
ahora, cuando menos, las verdaderas intenciones de Moscú pue-
den ser interpretadas como un anhelo de paz, combinada con
la preparación para defender el país y sus intereses contra un
ataque. Como Karl Radek dice, es probable que, en caso de
una nueva guerra mundial, la Rusia Soviética simplemente sea
un espectador, en lugar de tomar parte en ella.
En cuanto a la relativa importancia de las fronteras oc-
cidental y oriental para Rusia, Lenín personalmente dijo,
en 1920, a Katsuji Fusé, entonces corresponsal de "Tokio
Nichi-Nichi" en Moscú, que el verdadero comunismo podía
tener éxito solamente en el Occidente, y en 1925 le dijo tam-
bién que el Occidente era el frente de la U. S. S. R., y el Orien-
te su retaguardia. Si este parecer aún subsiste, Rusia debe
estar pensando más en el frente occidental que en el oriental.
Su atención, con todo, variará entre los dos frentes, de acuer-
do con la relativa importancia de la siutación que prevalezca
en uno y otro lado.
La política del Japón no debe consistir ni en exagerar ni
en menospreciar la amenaza rusa en el Oriente; pues exage-
rándola nos llevaría a tomar una actitud intransigente para
con las cuestiones pendientes, lo que inevitablemente significa-
ría la erupción de hostilidades; y, menospreciándola, por el
127

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.97)
contrario, nos conduciría a concentrar nuestra atención en los
asuntos chinos, e ignorar los alineamientos diplomáticos del
Soviet. Lo que tenemos que hacer es no omitir esfuerzo alguno
para arreglar las disputas pendientes con la U. S. S. R., mien-
tras al mismo tiempo fortalecemos nuestra posición diplomá-
tica y militar, para hacer frente a cualesquiera contingencias.
("El Universal", 16 de septiembre de 1936.)
128

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.99)
El Alma del Japón
Qué es el Japón? Esta es la cuestión que se ha planteado
repetidas veces. Siempre que el Japón viene a la delantera,
en conexión con algún acontecimiento, no importa en qué par-
te del mundo, la misma pregunta se insinúa en la mente oc-
cidental.
Algunos dicen que el Japón ha sido completamente occi-
dentalizado desde el siglo pasado. Aquellos que conocen el
Japón por las guías de viajero y por las fotografías, o que
han visto este país con sus propios ojos, sostienen por lo gene-
ral la misma opinión. Además, los extranjeros que han hecho
un estudio del Japón, declaran generalmente que el Japón no
tiene ninguna cultura que pueda llamarse suya propia; pues,
a su modo de ver, la introducción del budismo puso rna ca-
racterística budista sobre la cultura japonesa, y la civilización
china fué trasplantada en su integridad en suelo japonés, y
echó raíces y creció vigorosamente, mientras la importación
de la civilización occidental también podía significar única-
mente una cosa: la occidentalización del Japón. Todo esto no
es sino una verdad a medias. Los observadores que hacen ta-
les afirmaciones no pueden substraerse al cargo de ver la su-
129

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
perficie solamente. Es cierto que la apariencia exterior del
Japón ha cambiado casi completamente, cuando menos en dos
ocasiones, como resultado de la influencia exterior; sin em-
bargo, hay una cosa que permanece inalterada, y es el alma
del Japón. Una verdadera comprensión de esta quintaesencia
es la clave de la japonología.
Qué es, entonces, el alma del Japón? Es el profundo y
constante sentimiento de gratitud y felicidad con que la na-
ción japonesa, en conjunto, ha respetado, desde los tiempos
más remotos, la santidad del Emperador, la dignidad de la
Casa Imperial y la gloria del carácter fundamental de su Im-
perio. Se puede asegurar, sin peligro de equivocarse, que nin-
guna otra nación del mundo es más consciente de su naciona-
lidad que la japonesa, que ni por un momento se olvida de las
relaciones que existen entre el Soberano y el súbdito, entre el
gobernante y el gobernado. Ese sentido de la constitución na-
eional es el alma del Japón. Ahora bien; examinemos, aunque
sea someramente, la historia del Japón, para enseñar hasta qué
grado esta alma resultó afectada por la introducción del bu-
dismo, del confucionismo y de la civilización europea.
La Historia fija la época de la llegada del budismo en 552
A. D., aproximadamente, en el reino del Emperador Kimmel.
Hablando de la cultura, la introducción de esta religión fué un
provecho inmenso. Infundido como era con la civilización de
China, Persia y la India, el budismo se acreditó en el Japón
por se rel constructor de la espléndida cultura del período
de Nara (710-784), particularmente brillante en el dominio de
artes y oficios. Algunas de las obras de arte producidas en
aquel período, se conservan aún en "Shosoin", la mundialmen-
te famosa casa de tesoros de Nara. Al examinar estos inapre-
ciables objetos, no puede uno menos que admitir cuán bene-
130

=== Page 466 === (confidence 1.00)
ficiosa fué la introducción del budismo y de la civilización de
la Dinastía Tang, para elevar la cultura del Japón.
En lo que respecta al alma del Japón, sin embargo, la im-
portación del budismo hizo más daño que bien. El pueblo del
Japón antiguo, antes de tener contacto con esta religión, se
caracterizaba por su simplicidad, su alegría y una inelinación
optimista de espíritu. Mas el lado pesimista, negativo y pa-
tético del budismo, no dejó de afectar su modo de ver acerca
de la vida. Tan así fué que la política de la época, y en ver-
dad todas las esferas de actividades sociales e intelectuales,
quedaban bajo su influencia.
En pos del budismo, la civilización china llegó a nuestras
costas de una manera integral. El confucionismo, o sistema
chino de filosofía, fué también una de aquellas cosas que an-
taño había cruzado los mares hacia el Nipón. Pero durante
la Edad Media permaneció inactivo, hasta que Iyeyasu (1542-
1616), fundador del Shogunato Tokugawa y gran patrón de
sabiduría y letras, erudición, etc., hizo que las obras clásicas
de Confucio se imprimeran. En los primeros días del perío-
do de Yedo, el estudio de estas clásicas estuvo en su apogeo y
tuvo la virtud de neutralizar la mala influencia que ejercía el
budismo. Empero, como es un sistema de filosofía que se había
desarrollado en el suelo chino, fertilizado con los toques y ca-
racterística de aquella nación, el confucionismo naturalmente
tiene algo incompatible con el espíritu japonés. Por consecuen-
cia natural, resultó finalmente tan nocivo como fué útil en su
etapa inicial.
El shintoísmo quedó eclipsado por el budismo y el confu-
cionismo, pero en el Shinto vivía siempre el alma del Japón.
Muchos literatos se esforzaron por erigir un nuevo Japón, por
estudiar la política, la vida y la mentalidad de sus compatrio-
131

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
tas de los días anteriores al período de Nara. Con este fin
acogieron a las clásicas japonesas, tales como el Kojiki y el
Nihonshoki. Hirata Atsutané (1776-1843) fué el más avanza-
do investigador y hasta dió un paso adelante para convertir
estas indagaciones en acción, abriendo así el camino para la
gran restauración de 1868. Sus estudios comprendieron, ade-
más de las clásicas japonesas, el budismo, confucionismo,
taoísmo y hasta la Biblia.
Una de las más importantes doctrinas propuestas por estos
estudiantes de la sabiduría nativa del Japón, fué la de la divi-
nidad del Tenno (Emperador) y su evidente derecho a recla-
mar la absoluta obediencia de cada uno de los súbditos japo-
neses. Su argumento era que la lealtad de un japonés no se
debía tanto a su inmediato jefe o Shogún, como al Descen-
diente de la Diosa Sol, y que cosas tales como el amor propio
y la facción no deberían, por lo tanto, hallar lugar en el sis-
tema político del país.
Tan altos y fuertes fueron esos propagandistas shintoístas
en su encomio de las virtudes del Tenno. y tanto insistieron
ellos en las bien marcadas relaciones entre el Soberano y el
pueblo, que sus doctrinas resultaron ser hostiles a la existen-
cia del Shogunato. Naturalmente, los exponentes de las doc-
trinas shintoístas fueron vistos con enfado por el Shogunato,
y muchos de ellos tuvieron difeultades con el Gobierno de
Shogún.
La otra influencia que pesó sobre el pueblo japonés fué
la de la civilización europea, que hizo su incursión en el Im-
perio japonés después de la Restauración de Meiji. No conde-
namos la introducción de esta civilización. En efecto, las ame-
nidades materiales que hizo posible el occidentalismo, trajeron
muchos bienes. En sí misma, la civilización europea estuvo le-
132

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.99)
jos de ser deprimente para el alma del Japón. Solamente el
individualismo, que forma la idea central de la cultura oeei-
dental, no armoniza con el alma del Japón; y así, el hecho de
que la idea individualista mantenida en la Constitueión, en
las leyes y en general en las estructuras económico-políticas
del Japón, dió lugar a toda clase de problemas graves. El Ja-
pón ha estado, y aún está, luchando por resolver tales proble-
mas.
Es de desear que los estudiantes extranjeros que quieran
conocer el Japón verdadero, se penetren en el fondo de todaa
estas circunstancias, a fin de ver lo que es el alma del Japón,
y cómo ha resistido las influencias extranjeras en el pasado, y
ha de resistirlas en el futuro. En este sentido es altamente
satisfactorio saber que recientemente se ha hecho una traduc-
ción alemana del Kojiki, añadiendo así al acervo del conoei-
miento, ya enriquecido por las rebuscas de Aston, Chamber-
lain, Florenz y otros estudiantes eruditos. Tales estudios pro-
porcionarán una exacta y plena apreciación de aquellas fuer-
zas permanentes de nuestra vida nacional, sin la cual nadie
podrá comprender al Japón en el sentido real.
("El Universal", 21 de septiembre de 1936.)

nclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 69, dated July 27, 1937,
from the American Consulate General, Mexico, D. F., Mexico,
entitled "Japanese Activities in Lower California.
Report Dated July 23. 1937, from Consul William A.
Smale regarding the Activities of Japanese in
Lower California
The following information, although being as com-
prehensive as I can make it at'the moment, is incomplete,
due to the fact that accurate data can be obtained only
over a long period of time, and through sources of in-
formation not readily tapped.
I have been most secretive and discreet in my investi-
gation, and I have had to be so probably more than would be
necessary in any other part of Mexico, for the reason that,
in my opinion, in no other part of the Republic have there
been such numbers of Japanese operating with a view to
economic penetration and motivated by a desire to render a
military service to their country.
Owing to the fact that the Northern Territory of Baja
California, especially on the seacoast, is comparatively
free from routine surveillance of local authorities in many
sections, to the fact that from a military standpoint it is
most strategically situated with respect to the United
States, to the fact that it offers a splendid base for the
smuggling of aliens into the United States, and to the fact
that its waters abound in marine products for which the
Japanese have shown a great interest, persons of this race
located here, doing business here, or passing through here
have been more obscure in their operations than in other
parts of Mexico, wi th the possible exception of their opera-
tions during the last year or two within the Consular dis-
tricts of Guaymas and Mazatlán.
It will be recalled that in my letter of June 17, 1937,
I suggested that all persons of Japanese race irrespective
of their aatual citizenship might be included in the statis-
tics. Investigation has convinced me that so far as the
activities of persons of Japanese race who have acquired
Mexican citizenship are concerned, these persons in a
majority of cases are contributing more to the country of
tneir origin than those who have not acquired Mexican citi-
zenship. It is my firm belief that the acquisition of
Mexican citizenship by a Japanese resident of Mexico does
not cause him to forfeit any right or prestige in Japan or
from his government. As a matter of fact, there is reason
to believe that owing to the fact that the Mexican consti-
tution specifically precludes acquisition of territory by
aliens within the maritime and border zones, and to my sur-
mise that the Jepanese Government wishes its nationals to
gain as firm a foothold as possible on the West Coast of
Mexico I am left with the conviction that acquisition of
Mexican citizenship by persons of Japanese race may be
definitely approved of and encouraged by the Japanese
Government. To support this belief I may cite one fact and
one incident. The fact is that the principal officers of
the Japanese Association both in Ensenada and in Tijuana
are and have been for years Mexican citizens of Japanese
race. Tne incident, the accuracy of which I have checked,
and which is no doubt indicative of similar incidents of
frequent occurrence, is the following. At a meeting of
persons of Japanese race in a chilli drying shed in the
Maneadero Valley last year, a Mexican was sufficiently in-
quisitive as to creep to the rear of the shed unobserved.
Through

=== Page 471 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Through a crack in the shed he saw the occupants salaaming
before a Japanese whom he described as being in full dress
uniform of an Admiral or Colonel. Since the observer was
unable to distinguish between naval and military rank, it
is supposed that the most he could know is that this
Japanese (known to me) wore a uniform with obvious designa-
tion of rank. The important fact, however, is that this
individual had acquired Mexican citizenship previously.
In support of my belief that the Japanese Government
itself may be fostering, sponsoring, or even subsidizing
the acquisition of military information on the West Coast
of Mexico, I cite the following unrelated incidents.
From a Mexican citizen, a master mariner who has
served variously on Mexican government vessels and Mexican
and American coastwise and fishing vessels, who has been
an officer of the Mexican Naval Reserve, and whom I have
known since the days of my childhood spent in Ensenada, I
have received reports which would indicate that Japanese
persons have sought of him maps, charts, and data which
clearly were desired for military reasons. For example,
one Japanese, a Mexican citizen by naturalization, sought
of my acquaintance information respecting the condition of
the terrain on Guadalupe Island. My acquaintance also re-
ported the existence of a night class at Yavaros, Sonora,
allegedly conducted for the purpose of teaching Japanese
residents the Spanish language. The instructors and pupils
were Japanese. He reported that a close friend of his hed
been vhere he could observe the conduct of the class un-
noticed and was impressed b the fact that only the Jenanese
language was used in the class and that the study had been
almost exclusively of charts and maps. I, myself, have
observed Japanese pacing off distances between extreme
points of coves, or between well-defined land marks within
my consular district at times when I have come upon them
during hunting expeditions or while on outings with my
family.
The masters of sone American fishing vessels whom I
have gotten to know quite well during the almost seven
years in which I have been at Ensenada have assured me
most emphatically that they believe that various of the
officers of Japanese fishing vessels operating out of San
Diego and San Pedro are members of the Japanese Naval
Reserve, constantly alert and seeking information of
military value to their government. It is quite apparent
to me that this desire to obtain information is not restric-
ted to those who occupy an office in the Japanese military
or naval reserve. One senses it among practically all of
the Japanese within this area. The Japanese of the dis-
trict hold frequent meetins, the import of which, of
course, I do not know. Strangely enough, less than a
month ago when I called upon the Sub-Delegado in the
Maneadero Valley, ostensibly to discuss the agrarian
situation, but with a view to getting certain information
respecting Japanese land owmers which I knew he possessed,
and would be glad to give to me, the Sub-Delegado informed
me that through his contact with Japanese in the Maneadero
Valley and previously in the State of California he knew
them to be highly suspicious and constantly alert. He
stated that had I been observed by neighboring Japanese
approaching his ranch, this fact would constitute a reason
for the calling of a meeting. I learned later that a meet-
ing of the Japanese ranchers in the Maneadero Valley
followed

=== Page 472 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
followed shortly after my visit. Of course I cannot know
what business was conducted at the meeting. It may very
well be that its purpose was only to discuss the existing
perplexing agrarian situation.
In the opinion of the writer mere statistics do not
portray the threat of the Japanese on the West Coast of
Mexico. I am more impressed by their activities than by
the mere force of numbers. I can conceive that in some
localities of the interior of Mexico a comparatively high
Japanese agricultural population might little concern the
United States Government. In my consular district, however,
I find them to be as persistent as gravity. They seem to
be unrelentingly endeavoring to establish a foothold in
agriculture and in any industry connected with the sea, as
well as seeking information which would be of value to
tneir government in the event o hostilities with the
United States. It is true that at times their efforts have
been only partially successful but the pressure is always
present and at no time has a setback eliminated previous
gain. They have an apparently racial characteristic of
being willing to look to a rather distant future and to be
willing to accept with satisfaction any progress, no matter
how slight it may be.
The Japanese have used their fishing activities in
Mexico as a means of introducing their nationals into the
United States in violation of our inmigration laws. The
records of the Consulate indicate that in the calendar
year 1924 there were 380 Japanese listed on alien crew
lists visaed at this consulate, and that since that time
through June 30, 1937, 1170 new names have appeared on
crew lists visaed at this office. At the present time
there are probably no more than 140 Japanese employed on
fishing vessels obtaining crew lists presented at the
Ensenada Consulate. Thus there remain to be accounted
for 12lo Japanese who have been employed on vessels pro-
ceeding to the United States since January 1, 1924, and
who are no longer so employed. Allowing for a margin of
210 to cover possiple discrepencies in the spelling of
names and to care for those Japanese who have left their
vessels to follow another pursuit ashore, or who may have
returned to Japan, it is my belief that practically the
entire remaining 1000 former seamen have entered the United
States in violation of our immigration laws. To this num-
ber, of course, may be added an undetermined but large num-
ber of Japanese who have never been listed on alien crew
lists viased at this offioe, but who may have been smuggled
into the United States from Baja California during this
period of time.
It is not my contention that these Japanese have left
the vessels on a voyage on which their names appeared as
members of the crew. During these voyages, however, they
have had the opportunity of learning some English, of pur-
chasing American clothing, of becoming acquainted witn
California localities and conditions in order that when
they later enter the United States surreptitiously they may
more easily escape detection. I have discussed this matter
at length and often with immigration inspectors in charge
and immigration border patrol officers, and all have agreed
that it is not a difficult matter for a Japanese not listed
on a crew list to proceed as a passenger on a fishing vessel
departing from Baja California and to be transferred to a
small Japanese fresh fish boat prior to arrival of the former
vessel at a California port. The smaller vessel, it is to
be

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
be remembered, is not subject to immigration or customs
examination unless under suspicion. My contentions in this
regard are outlined in a letter to the District Director of
Immigration at Los Angeles, dated April 20, 1936, which
accompanied my despatch number 1189 of Apri1 21, 1936. A
copy of this letter is enclosed herewitn for ready reference.
When I first came to Ensenada in the latter part of
1930, I immediately suspected that the Ensenada Consular
district was being used by Japanese as a base for smuggling
themselves into the United States. Consequently I took
extreme measures to restrict the signing on of new Japanese
crews on vessels proceeding from Ensenada to the United
States either through the fishing grounds or directly.
For example, during 1924 there were 380 different Japanese
listed as members of crews on fishing vessels proceeding
to the United States. In 1925 291 new names appeared. In
1926 231 new names appeared. In 1927 230 additional names
appeared. In 1931 and 1932 these figures had fallen to 39
and 38 respectively. This I attripute chiefly to the fact
that I discovered ways in whicn I could show a justification
for a refusal to visa crew lists of vessels signing on
Japanese seamen in Lower California who had not previously
been members of crews proceeding to the United States.
While it is barely possiple that my reasons thus to restrict
the former activitiy were not entirely justifiable under the
existing laws and regulations, they have not been contested
and therefore have been effective. Unfortunately, however,
once the policy of the Consulate had been definitely estab-
lished and it became known that only in rare instances could
Japanese who had only recently arrived in Baja California
from Japan join vessels proceeding to the United States,
they naturally sought a means of circumventing the restric-
tions imposed at my office and were successful in doing so
in the following manner. Masters of vessels desiring to
replace members of the crew by Japanese who had not been
employed previously proceed to Turtle Bay or Magdalena Bay
where the new Japanese seamen are shipped. They then go
to the fishing grounds and later to San Diego, California,
without touching at Ensenada. The immigration inspector
in char e at San Diego ordered these newly employed Japan-
ese to be detained on board the vessel, and deported.
Nevertheless, following a comparatively short period of
employment on these vessels, the Japanese were then in most
instances iven seamen'ε identification cards. Once in
possession of these cards, the Consulate did not consider
itself justified in refusing to certify crew lists bearing
the names of such Japanese, and from then on the vessels
were free to call at Ensenada to obtain crew list visas.
I consider it important to state that masters of vessels
involved repeatedly have admitted to me that, although
the Japanese seamen to which I refer were ordered detained
aboard their vessels and deported to Mexico, detention in
most cases was not enforced.
In confirmation of my telegram of today, the final
figures in response to your original letter of May 13, 1937,
are as follows. Japanese national& residing in the Ensenada
Consular district--550. This figure includes only persons
of Japanese race and nationality. There are approximately
loo more who through naturalization or birth in Mexico are
Mexican citizens, but who are of unmixed Japanese race.
Entrants into the Ensenada district during the last two
years are estimated to be 135 which includes 35 who have
entered

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
entered the district legally and 1o0 who are estimated to
have arrived illegally, chiefly from vessels which have
touched at Cedros Island or Turtle Bay.*
In arriving at the figures shown above, I have been
able to use the following lists:
1. A list of Japanese kept by the Mexican immigration
office in Ensenada. This list contained 274 names.
2.

by the secretary of the Japanese Association in
Ensenada. This list contained the names of 232

United States imnigration and naturalization serv-

at San Isidro, covering Japanese who have passed in
transit through the United States between San Fran-
cisco and San Isidro under bond. There were 22
such individuals.
5.
A list of Japanese who are leasing land from
American ranchers in the Maneadero Valley. There
are 20 persons in this list.
6.
A list of 338 Japanese seamen whose names appear

the past two years. I estimated that approximately
100 of these seamen have smuggled into the United
States by the present time. It is inown that some
of those listed more than a year ago have left their
vessels, and their names do not appear on the crew
lists of other vessels.
7.
A list of 136 Japanese appearing in the 1937
Japanese-American directory published by the Japanese-
American News in Japan. This list follows sufficient-
ly closely the list prepared by the Japanese Associa-
tion in Ensenada that it may be considered to have
been compiled by the Association.
From the 971 names appearing in the list I have pre-
pared 514 cards. Ooviously there have been many duplica-
tions with the result that only 514 different names appeared
in the various lists. Certain deductions by reason of the
fact that certain persons listed are known to be Mexican
citizens, and a deduction to cover probable discrepancy in
the speliing of names, and additions to cover an estimated
number of residents in the Tijuana area have been made to
bring avout the totals telegraphed to the Consulate General.
It is my belief that my estimates for the Tijuana area have
been fairly accurate since I have had the Japanese directory,
the list of United States immiration border crossing cards,
and a knowledge that there are 85 members of the Japanese
Association in Tijuana, to aid me.

As a sidelight on the activities of Japanese interests

Copies of these letters were furnished me by the Manager
of the Marine Products Conmany in support of his conten-
tion that the firm of Mitsui & Company intends to dominate
the abalone industry of Mexico and probably other phases
of the marine proaucts industry. The Paciric Ocean Products
Ltd. apparently is suosidized or controlled by the Japanese
firm of Mitsui & Company and is operating a newly established
abalone cannery at Turtle Bay, Baja California.
With respect to the seemingly reat facilities and
encoura ement given to Japanese fishing interests oy the
Mexican Government, the Consulate General is informed that
the consensus of opinion among persons engaged in fishing
activities in tnis vicinity is that most of the help has
come irom one person in the Mexican Government--Sr. Camiro,
nead of the Servicio Pesca Maritima in the Departmento
Forestal y de Caza y Pesca.

COPY
Ensenada, B. C., Mexico, April 20, 1936.
Strictly Confidential.
District Director of Immigration,
Los Angeles, California.
Sir:
The Consulate refers to its strictly confiden-
tial 1etter of April 18, 1936, in which you were in-
formed of the intention of the chief of the local
immigration office to call all Japanese subjects
within his jurisdiction into his office for the
purpose of permitting him to review their documents
and pass upon their immigration status. The possi-
vility was suggested that those Japanese not proper-
ly documented in accoruance with Mexican laws might
either attenpt to hide within the district or
smuggle themselves into the United States.
Reference also is made to a letter which this
office directed to the Inspector in Charge, Immi-
gration and Naturalization Service, at San Diego,
under date of April 18, 1936, a copy of which was
furnished you. In this communication it was re-
ported that information had been received to the ef-
fect that Cedros Island, Baja California, may be a
base for the smuggling of aliens, narcotics, and al-
cohol.
As being of interest in connection with the
matters discussed in the letters referred to above,
the Consulate has to report that on the morning of
April 16, 1936, Oren H. Dickason, master of the
American oil screw SENATOR of Los Angeles, Cali-
fornia, called at this office to discuss the possi-
bility of engaging a crew of Japanese fishermen at
Ensenada on his next trip to the fishing grounds.
He stated that his desire to secure a crew of Jap-
anese at this port or, if necessary, elsewhere in
Baja California, was influenced entirely by his in-
ability to hire trained fishermen at San Pedro, and
his unwillingness to "break in" inexperienced men.
Not only was the Consulate unable to agree with
Captain Dickason's contention that a crew of able
fishermen could not be obtained from among the un-
employed Americans or legally admitted aliens at
San Pedro or San Diego, but in the light of ex-
perience gained during the past several years it
felt quite emphatically that he would experience
considerable difficulty in finding a group of
Japanese in this district who could convince this
office that they were bona fide seamen and non-
immicrants within the meaning of the immigration
laws. He was assured that this office would make a
careful and thorough examination of each alien with
a view to determining whether he could be, properly
classified as a seaman having a domicile in this
country to which he would be permitted to return.
I may add that inquiry, made prior and subsequent
to the visit of Captain Dickason discloses a con-
sensus of opinion among masters of fishing vessels
calling at this port that suitable fishermen are
available in the United States provided a reason-
able wage or share is given them, and that the un-
controlled signing on of Japanese fishermen in Baja
California

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
California would tend to throw the fishing industry
of California out of balance and operate as an un-
fair disadvantage to vessels wishing to retain crews
comprised of American citizens or lawfully admitted
aliens.
During the course of the discussion, Captain
Dickason expressed the belief, based upon a con-
versation which he alleges to have had with a
friend in the United States Immigration and Natural-
ization Service, that if he were to take aboard a
crew of Japanese fishermen at some "no consul" port
and were to proceed with them to San Pedro directly
from the fishing grounds he would experience no
difficulty in having them passed by the immigration
authorities as seamen and that subsequent entrances
into the American port would be effected with equal
ease. When informed that he might be required to
place a bond of $1,Ooo for any alien member of the
crew found not to be a bona fide seaman, Captain
Dickason admitted that he was aware of that fact.
It might be well to mention that in connection
with the examination at this office of Japanese
residents of the Ensenada consular district to de-
termine their admissibility into the United States
either as non-immigrants or as seamen, the fact
that the alien is able to show that he legally
entered Mexico is not taken as proof that he has
in this country a legal domicile because it has
been found that many applicants of that race have
not complied with the laws of Mexico relating to
the registration of aliens. It is the Consulate's
understanding that an alien who has failed to
register in accordance with existing law could be
refused readmission into Mexico.
Despite the Consulate's attempt to discourage
the master of the oil screw SENAToR from endeavoring
to secure an alien crew at this or another port of
Baja California, the vessel arrived at Ensenada from
San Diego on the morning of April 19, 1936, with a
crew composed of the following American citizens;
Oren Dickason, Master.
James Hodges, Engineer.
Fred Gratto, Assistant Engineer.
Harmon Farr, Radio Operator.
At the time of the last visit to this port of
the O/S SENATOR, on January 5, 1936, it carried a
crew of eleven men, including the master.
According to the master's signed application
for a consular bill of health, the vessel was to
leave this port on the afternoon of April 19th bound
for Los Angeles via the fishing Crounds. It has been
subsequently learned that it is the master's in-
tention to take on a crew of Japanese somewhere in
the vicinity of Cedros Island or at Turtle Bay, Baja
California. It is assumed that the skeleton crew
will be augmented by the shipment of at least seven
aliens.
In

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
In view of the information which this office
has received respecting the intention of the local immi-
gration authorities to examine carefully the status
of every Japanese resident of this district, and the
belief of our own officials that Cedros Island is a
base for the smuggling of aliens, etc., the Consulate
is forced to look with suspicion upon the contem-
plated action of the master of the O/S SENATOR.
Needless to say, any success which the Oon-
sulate may have had during the past five years and
any success which it may achieve in the future
in controlling the shipment of alien seamen at
this port will be rendered valueless if masters of
vessels are permitted to engage aliens at ports
outside of the jurisdiction of a consulate with
any reasonable expectation of impunity. If such
a practice were to be established not only would
Americans and legally admitted aliens soon be dis-
placed by the cheap labor thus obtained, but it is
also reasonable to assume that alien Japanese in
the vicinity of Cedros Island, Turtle Bay, Magda-
lena Bay, etc., who are desirous of entering the
United States clandestinely, would welcome the
opportunity of joining an American fishing vessel
and later, when the time seemed propitious, en-
deavor to leave the vessel at an American port.
Moreover, those who are able to join fishing ves-
sels at one of the points named, put who have no
definite or immediate desire of illegally entering
the United States, will, if permitted the priv-
ilege of going ashore while in an American port,
be in a position to acquaint thenselves with the
ceography and other details of that section of
the country pertaining to the port of call and thus
secure information whic: at some later date may be
employed to convince American immigration authori-
ties that they entered the United States on some
"forgotten" vessel prior to 1924, which, if the
Consulate's understanding is correct, would mean
that our autnorities might find it difficult to
secure the aliens' deportation.
Judging from the tenor of casual inquiries
made by masters of other American fishin vessels
respecting the possiple employment of local Japan-
ese fishermen, the Consulate is firmly of the
opinion that should the vessel under discussion
succeed in securing a fisning crew at Cedros Island
or Turtle Bay, as the case may be, and subsequently
experience no great diriiculty upon entering an
American port, it will not be long before other
American vessels follow its example. It is be-
lieved that vigorous action by the immigration
authorities in the present case would effectively
curb and discourage the shipment of alien seamen
at "no consul" ports on the cast of Baja California,
with the result that the Consulate will be in a
position to continue its close surveillance of the
activities of Japanese seamen engaged on fishing
vessels operating in these Mexican waters.
Very truly yours,
WM. A. SMALE,
American Consul.

Cia. de Productos Marinos, S. A.
Ensenada, B. C., Mexico
Dear Sirs:
Attn: Sr. Miguel Candara, Gen. Mgr.
Shin Shibata was in this office Saturday and stated that
he came in to see if some arrangement could be made where-
by a price agreement could be effected petween this company
and Mitsui & Co.
The writer explained to him that Mitsui's position is so
entirely difrerent that there is no chance to arrive at
any price agreement, inasmuch as Mr. Morehead has already
sold to Mitsui at approximately 35 per case less than any
of the American exporters can buy it. Shibata finally
admitted that Mitsui did have an advantage of price in
the Orient.
The fishing problem generally was discussed and during the
course of the conversation, he stated that Dr. Mitsui, one
of the Japanese experts who has just arrived in Mexico, is
making a trip up the West Coast, investigating the abalone
and fishing industry cenerally, for the Mexican government,
and will make recomnendations, as he understands it, to
the Fish & Game Department in Mexico for laws regulating
the industry.
He further stated that the Mexican government is now build-
ing in Japan two 1arge fishing trawlers to cost $500,000.00
(iN) each and that 15 or 20 young Mexican boys are leaving
for Japan to be instructed in the fishing industry and to
come home with the S boats which the government is building.
You probably have talked to Mr. Shipata the last few days,
and if so, you will notice his independent attitude, and as
a matter of fact, he stated very frankly that he does
not feel the Japanese have anythin to fear in Lower
California. This information is passed on to you for what
it may be worth.
Without anything more for the moment and with best wishes
from the writer, we are
Very truly yours,
LARINE PRODUCTS COLPANY
By
L. K. SHALL.

Pacific Ocean Products, Ltd.

Dear Sir:
Referring to our recent conversation in your office con-
cerning canned abalone.
It was my understanding that under no circumstances were
you in favor of cutting prices and that you would in no
event sell to any exporter at below our opening price of
$7.40 net cash, in lots of 1000 cases or over.
On Thursday last, Mr. Shin Shibata was in this office
and during the conversation, informed me that you, or your
company, had executed, as exclusive Sales Representatives
of Coronado Fisheries, now packing at Bahia Tortuga, B.C.,
Mexico, a contract with the Japanese exporting firm of
Mitsui & Co., San Francisco, under the terms of which they
are to receive a large part, if not all, of the 1937 pack
of abalone from the Turtle Bay cannery, at a price which
figures $6.75 net per case, unlabeled, ex warehouse Los
Angeles, Harbor.
After allowing a normal cost for labeling, handling,
strapping, etc., it is obvious that Mitsui & Co. have been
given a net price which figures 35 cents or 40 cents per
case less than any American exporter in San Francisco has
purchased a case of abalone during the past two years, and
despite the fact that Mitsui & Co. have never handled any
Mexican abalone before, this places them in a most enviable
position insofar as price is concerned in the Orient.
If I have been misinrormed concerning this matter, will
you kindly be good enough to set me right in order that I
may advise the two Mexican pacing plants vith whom we
have contracts and likewise so that the American exporters
in San Fr ncisco may know what to expect in the way of com-
petition from their Japanese conpetitor on canned abalone
in the Orient.
Your kind response will be sincerely appreciated.
Yours very truly,
MARINE PRODUCTS COMPANY
By
L. K. Small.

CORONADO FISHERIES, INO.
410 S. Main St.
LOS ANGELES
June 14, 1937
Mr. L. K. Small
Marine Products Co.
San Diego, Calif.
Dear Sir:
Your letter of June 8 has been held for my attention.
In my discussions and negotiations with Mitsui & Co.,
Ltd., the importance of the price structure in the abalone
market was fully recognized, and I am confident that our
sales representatives will strictly adhere to the policy
of maintaining a firm price level. The realization of
profits is the purpose of all business activity and
nothing is more harmful to accomplishing this objective
than a price war.
I do not mind telling you that our deal was made at
$7.50 per case, less the usual five percent commission,
and the normal allowance for the assumption by the pur-
chaser for cash payment, warehousing charges, insurance,
labeling, and strapping.
I see no reason why our sales representatives should
sacrifice the normal and legitimate profit to which they
are entitled by upsetting conditions in the market in the
Orient.
I sincerely trust that our entrance into the abalone
market will permit of cordial and profitable relations with
everyone interested in the business.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) Win. C. Morehead
President

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.99)
SUSPICIONS AROUSED
Corresposident Views With Alarm
Aliem Activities in Our Country
To the Editor of The Union
Sir: We want, in this country, no
military training camps under the
auspices of foreign governments or
alien individuals.
At the present time such camps, it
is belleved, have been established in
various parts of the country and I
consider them a menace to our Gov-
ernment. What does it mean when
the German Bund has a number of
such camps in operation now? It
would be interesting. to know whether
Herman von Busch, Bund state sec-
retary of New Jersey, is an American
citizen or not.
Why are Germans and Italians
compelled to register their names with
the consulates of these countries?
There is a growing suspicion that
in case of war with these countries
these trained bunds would be used to
form a hostile army in our midst.
Also how about the many Japanese
in California? In case of a war with
that nation they would be a great
hazard, hard to overcome before this
Nation could raise an army of equal
or superior numbers and transport
it to California.
The Japanese have always coveted
California and some years ago tried
to secure from Mexico a base for a
naval station at Magdalena Bay on
the peninsula of Old California, but
fortunately for us Mexico refused to
allow this.
At the present time the Japanese
are seeking a concession from Mex-
ico to establish a port at Ensenada
for the alleged purpose of operating
a steamship service to Honolulu. En-
senada is only about 60 miles south
of San Diego and would be a fine
place for the Japanese to establish
an airport from which they could at-
tack the American cities on the Cali-
fornia coast.
Why do I think of these possibili-
ties? It is because Japan, Germany
and Italy are warlike nations and
the United States has the world's
supply of gold and if Japan and Ger-
many became allies and one attacked
us on the west coast and the other
on the east, the United States would
be between two millstones, seeking to
crush us for the sake of our gold
and for the possession of California
as a fertile possession for Japan's
increasing population.
AN AMERICAN.
Springfield, Aug. 4, 1937.

ne had seen on February 13 a party of

Chihuahua some 250-300 miles south of the American

the Mexican Pacific coast line, is that they are

ing places, and possible airfield bases, information
for use in case of war between Japan and the United
States.
Mr. Collins states also that he made a report of
the incidents above related to the Clerk of the In-
telligence Department at Fort Bliss, Texas, on
February 10, 1938.
Mr. Collins

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
SUBJECT:
Japanese Activities in Baja California.

THE SECRETARY OF STATE.
WASHINGTON.
894.20212/3
SIR:
I have the honor to bring to the recollection
of the Department the fact that on previous occasions
the Consulate has reported suspicious activities of
Japanese who may have been engaged in the procurement
39
of information likely to be of militery interest to
the ir Go vernment.
The Department now is informed that other members
of the staff and I within the last few weeks have
noticed a number of Japanese, well-dressed, distinct-
ly not of the laboring cless, and not residents of
this district, roving about from place to place in
Ensenada and its vicinity, taking photographs and
making observations with the use of high-powered
field glasses. While my family and I were on a picnic
on a hill which overlooks Ensenada and Todos Santos
Bay, recently, two Japanese, one carrying a camera and
one a pair of field glasses, visited the locality of
F/FG
our camping place. They stated that they were residents
of

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of Ensenada, which they were not; attempted to
speak Spanish, which they could not; took photographs
of the surrounding country from various angles and
made observations and no tes. I borrowed the field
glasses which one of the Japanese carried and found
them to be very high powered, with a Zeiss lens in
a frame manufactured in Japan.
I recognize that this and other incidents of a
similar nature do not prove conslusively that Japanese
are engaged in espionage or in the gathering of mili-
tary information in this vicinity. On the other hand,
I consider that under the circumstances it would be
unwise to disregard entirely the possibility that
this type of activity is being and had been under-
taken.
Respectfully yours,
Wm. A. Smale,
American Consul.

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.98)
92390-XC
April 8, 1938.
Memorandum
A Jepanese newspeper under the name of "The New World, Inc."
ms originally establiahed in San Francisco in 1894, and the record
in the San Francisco post office shows its date of original entry
as second alass matter as of June 18, 1894. Its editor was indi-
cated as T. Abe, managing editor, R. Ninomiya, and business manager,
I. Sugmi, the owmers and only stookholders being New World, Inc.,
T. Abe and S. Yamagata.
On Movember 26, 1932, the name of the paper was changed to
"The New World Daily News", (ciroulation as of October 1932 -
13,z40). Its division of classification number in the office of
the Third Assistant Postmaster General at Washington was 196933,
changed to 33791 in 1923.
There existed at this time (1932) another Japanese newspaper
kmown as "The Hokubei Ambgi", (ciroulation as of October 1932 -
3,636) its publiaher and editor being K. Ebina and business manager,
S. Hare.
There is also another Japanese newspaper known as "The Japanese
American News Publishing Company" whose circulation in October l937
is indicated as 9,425. The editor is I. Kawashima, managing editor,
S. Aseno, business manager, I. Tauda; owmers, Japanese American
Hews Publishing Company and Ton& Abiko, all at 650 Ellis Street,
San Francisco; mortgage held by Zellerbach Paper Company, 534 Battery
Street, San Francisco.
On June 17, 1935, "The New World Daily News" and "The Hokubei
Ashai" ware consolidated and the name changed to "The New World-Sun",
Mr. S. Hara, general manager, having made the request. The first
issue of the newly named paper was dated June 20, 1925, the office
of publication being the same as that of "The New World Daily News",
1618 Geary Street, San Francisco.
The statement of owmerahip and management of "The New World-Sun"
submitted to the postmaster at San Francisco in October l937 shows
the following:
Circulation 950l; editor, T. Abe, managing editor, T. Ota,
business manager, K. Ichimaru, primipal stockholders, T.
Abe, K. Ebina, S. Hara, K. Hatakeyama and K. Okagari -
(address of all indicated as 16l8 Geary Street, San Fran-
cisco, California).

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Mr. William Prendergast, bookkeeper in charge of the second
elass mailings section of the San Francisco post office, informed
me that K. Ichimaru is more or less of a recent addition to the
paper; that he is apparently a most capable man of 35 to 40 years
of age, makes e very good appearance,and is of the athletic type,
and he is quite certain that Ichimaru is the editor-in-chief of
"The New World-Sum".
I took the matter up with Inspector Walsh of the Bureau of
Immigration and Naturalization at San Francisco and he checked with
his main office located on Angel Ialand in San Francisco Bay, ascer-
taining that the records there disclosed that Mr. Kakuzo Ichimaru
first entered the United States on the S. S. Siberia on May 12,
191s, at which time he was 12 years and 11 months of age and destined
to join his father at 655t Main Street, Seattle, Washington.
His file disclosed that Kakuzo Ichimaru again arrived in the
United States at Seattle, Washington, on March 13, 1924 (no record
of steemer, etc., at San Francisco).
The record further disclosed that on June 28, 1924, he again
arrived at San Francisco on the S. S. Korea Maru from Yokohame;
his age indicated as 25; male; married; newspaper reporter and
destined to Mr. Eigo Higashi, zoo0-A California Street, San Fran-
cisco; 5'6" in height, with a scar on his left hand; born at
Kumemoto Ken, Japan. He was accompanied by his wife Asae Ichimaru,
and his wife has applied for a reentry permit at San Francisco
under date of March 22, 1938, evidently intending to visit Japan.
The foregoing covers all of the information obtained con-
cerning Kakuso Ichimaru, who, according to Mr. Williem Prendergast
of the post office, is believed to be the editor-in-chief of "The
New World-Sum". It will be noted that as a boy of about 13 years
of age he arrived in this country and joined his father at 655t
Main Street, Seattle, Washington, and the record indicates that
he arrived at Seattle again on March 13, 1925. Any information
concerning how long he remained in Seattle between May of 1912
and March of l924 would be available at the U. S. Immigration
Office at Seattle. It is possible he returned to Japan to attend
school. As shown in the record, he again arrived in the hited
States on June 28, 1924, having made a fast trip to Japan between
Maroh and June of that year. There was nothing learned or dis-

=== Page 511 === (confidence 0.99)
3
alosed to indicate that he was connected with the Japanese Navy
Department, although he may be so oonnected.
Inammuch as Mr. S. Hara is shown as general manager of"The
New World-Sun" in his letter of June 17, 1935, copy herewith, I
deemed it advisable to obtain information concerning his status,
and Inspector Walsh of the U. S. Immigration Service furniahed
me with the following information:
Shintaro Hara arrived on Siberia Maru on May 28, 1926; 43
years old; married; accompanied by wife; business manager of
Japanese and American Press; destined to friend Mr. K. Abiko,
650 Ellis Street, San Francisco; 5 ft. 4 in.; mole end right
eye; in possession of reentry permit issued at Washington June
3, 1986 - No. 133437. His record shows former admission at
Seattle 1904; San Francisco directory, page 708, shows Japanese
American News Company, I. Kawashina, manager, address 650 Ellis
street.
The record of Mr. Shintaro Hara disclosed that his first
arrival in the United States was at Seattle, Washington, in
1904, and information concerning the particulars, if desired,
can be obtained from the U. S. Immigration Service at Seattle
or through the Commissioner of Immigration at Washington, D. C.
It will be noted that Hara's reentry permit was issued at Wash-
ington, and this permit, I am informed, carries his photograph
and full description and is on file in the office of the Com-
missioner of Immigration at Washington, D. C.
There is herewith a copy of the statement of owmerahip,
management, circulation, etc., of "The New World-Sun" dated
October 1, l937, and a copy of that newspaper, issue of April
6, 1938.
Respectfully submitted,
Rost Office Inspector.

=== Page 524 === (confidence 0.99)
ARMY AND NAVY BRANCH
YOUNG MEN'S CHRISTIAN ASSOCIATION
166 Embaraadero
San Francisco, California
March 26, 1938
Hon. Sumner D. Welles
Under Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
I feel that information which casually came to my
attention on the waterfront here in San Francisco may or
may not be authentic however you may accept it at its face
value for your official perogative in the present expropria-
3
tion by the Mexioan Government of the oil industry in Mexico.
There is a Japanese newspaper which I understand is called
the New World published here in this city. Its editor is as
I am informed the unofficial representative of the Imperial
Japanese Navy Dept. My information is that the present
am ition to have the oil or petroleum industry of Mexico
reverted or expropriated by that Govt dates back to the summer
of l935 on that occasion their visited Pacific Coast ports
several Japanese war vessels presumably a training cruise
which likewise had a mission of experts in its roster Naval
and civilian geologists and transportation experts. These
ships paid a visit to Agapulpo a port on the exican Pacific
Coast. This may not be the correct spelling of this port.
However this Commission had a lengthy discussion with the
Central Mexican Govt. regarding the present oil situation and
the Japanese Govt. offered an inducement to the Mexican

=== Page 525 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
State that they would finance the work of establishing the
requirements of pipe lines and harbour improvements for
oil transportation from the Mexican Pacific Coast. Much
detail has since consumated in the intricate work that has
so far developed. Other natural resources are alsò in this
scope.As a matter of fact I am told that there is a sizeable
group of Japanese operating in Mexico City and on the Mexican
Pacific Coast at present and have been working on this
present ambition for the past four years and its maturity was
and is still supposed to be piece meal until such time as the
Japanese Govt. Commission could guarantee the feasibility of
the original plan which now has assured itself to be workable.
I am of course only an electrician working on marine work on
San Francisco Bay and my pen is most humble therefore this
information may be just some more idle logic of which we now
have so much of. However since this was given to me two
years ago and developments are maturing according to original
information I am compelled to offer this humble story to my
government. At that tine I also was given much information by
a zealous German who told me of a German cruiser that visited
Alaskan waters in the guise of a friendly visit which actually
surveyed the entire coast and harbors of that territory and
had as its guests on board five high ranking Japanese naval
officers. Now sir if you consider this as warranting official
investigation you must accept my information for what it is
worth. However I will frankly recommend the utmost surveiliance
of this Editor of the Japanese paper New World for I under-

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
stand he far exceeds his position for the welfare of the
United States as a whole.
I am very truly yours,
(signed) Hubert F. Malone
